- **6.5m-sec-a-iii — SEC-009…SEC-015 checked against master: six confirmed, one partly fixed; 6.5m-sec-a closes.**
  - **Three probes, each run and deleted:**
    - **SEC-009:** a policy whose two witnesses share one note name parses, and both are labelled as the first.
    - **SEC-010:** a root-signed `params` entry mixing a valid and an invalid value is rejected, yet across 200 replays of one log it changed `rotate_needs_signer` 31 times. Go disagrees with itself, so two nodes on the same log can differ on whether a device-only rotate installs a key.
    - **SEC-011:** a single empty temporary file left by a power cut makes a reopened log come back at size 0.
  - **By reading:** SEC-012 (the node's own key in owner mode's desired set — the kernel half cannot be run on this Mac), SEC-013 (= F3), SEC-015 (the replay fuzzer opens a file name the fixture lost long ago and skips on every run). SEC-014 is partly fixed by 6.5l-vii-d-xii-iv; runbook R7 still calls rotation a remedy for a compromised log key.
  - **Bug fix or decision.** SEC-009, 010, 011, 012 and 015 make the implementations do what `SPEC.md` already says, and need no decision. SEC-014's verifier-side retirement would, and it will be raised as one; its runbook correction will not.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests; `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean. No code changed in this step.

- **6.5m-sec-a-ii — SEC-002…SEC-008 checked against master: two fixed, three partly fixed, and two confirmed holes that matter more than their labels.**
  - **The table** is under "AUDIT TRIAGE" with the decision blocks; every line was checked here, by reading master's code or with a throwaway probe that was deleted after running.
  - **The worst find: SEC-005 (a), rated medium, defeats the freeze outright.** A node holding a shorter branch and served the longer one raises FORK and freezes, but cannot build evidence. It writes no file, and after a restart it is no longer frozen. A forking log only has to show its victims the longer branch, which is the natural shape of a growing log. The branch fixed this in `bdb3086`; master never got it. It goes ahead of H2.
  - **SEC-004's remainder:** H1 made the head survive, not the clock. A view the same process called `degraded` two hours on is `fresh` again after a restart on the same head, because the witness clock starts at the first head it sees. And a restarted node asks no peers until something verifies, so a node rebooted while its log is down cannot use gossip.
  - **SEC-002:** the gossip half was fixed by 6.5l-vii-d-xii-ii. What is left is a wrong number in README G2: two k-of-N quorums share 2k−N witnesses, not k. There is also a policy nothing warns about, and a monitor that never compares heads.
  - **Fixed already:** SEC-006 (by H1-d), SEC-007 for nodes (by d-xii-ii). **Confirmed:** SEC-003 (H2), SEC-008 (no `revoke`, `rotate` or governance tooling, while the runbooks document them).
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests; `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean. No code changed in this step.

- **6.5m-sec-a-i — SEC-001 is real on master, in both implementations and in the vectors; a decision is raised and the documents stop claiming otherwise.**
  - **Read from the branch, not checked out:** `git show origin/claude/tender-mccarthy-bzllsp:docs/audit/SECURITY_AUDIT.md`. The claim: re-appending a past entry with one junk signature re-applies it, because `dup-entry` hashes raw bytes and `sigs` is outside every preimage.
  - **Reproduced** with throwaway probes, deleted after running and never committed. Go: `37-enroll-spare` plus one unresolvable `signer` signature appended at index 138 is accepted, and `spare` goes from revoked to **active**. `28-policy` the same way is accepted, and the quorum falls from 3-of-3 to **2-of-3**. The first probe's junk `kid` sorted first and was refused `decode`, because `sigs` must be sorted by `kid` (6.5k). Sorted last, it passes, as the audit says. The Python reference gives the same two results. In the shipped log, `70-profile-base` and `89-two-sig-base` share a preimage and are both accepted, so the suite pins the behaviour. The other repeated preimages in the fixture (1/13, 4/85, 0/113) are exact byte repeats and are refused `dup-entry`, which is D-031 working as written.
  - **Raised:** DECISION NEEDED — 6.5m-sec-001b, recommending that duplicates be keyed on the signed content (option A), which is what D-031 was for. Two rows follow from it: 6.5m-sec-001a, an interim mitigation that needs no decision, and 6.5m-sec-001b, the fix.
  - **Documents corrected now, not after the fix.** README G3 and `THREAT-MODEL.md` A2 ("Harmless") and G3 each carry an "open defect SEC-001" note. A security claim known to be false does not stay on the page while a decision is pending.
  - **The audit's "deferred by the maintainer"** cannot be traced to a decision by the owner. It is recorded as the branch's claim, not as a fact.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests; `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean. No code changed in this step.

- **6.5m-i-e — D-069 written down where each reader looks; H1 closes.**
  - **`DECISIONS.md` D-069.** What a node held outlives a restart and a reboot: the stored checkpoint (as the mirror), the keys and policy it learned, and fork evidence, all in a persistent state directory. The restored head is held and not enforced until something verifies. It records the four rejected readings (enforce at once; refuse to start on tmpfs; persist only the checkpoint; hash-named evidence) and the cost, including the one thing not yet true: the mirror surviving a power cut (H4-f).
  - **`SPEC.md` §13.2**, the paragraph a second implementer reads. These three persist across a restart and a reboot: the stored checkpoint, the learned keys and policy, and fork evidence. The persisted checkpoint is re-verified at startup, falling back to what its own entries publish after an in-log rotation. Every candidate is compared against it before anything is adopted, and it is not enforced until a checkpoint verifies in the new process. This is node behaviour under the decided D-069; no wire format and no vector changed.
  - **`ENFORCEMENT.md` §8**, the table an operator reads. The crash row now says a restarted node holds its head and freeze again. A new `host reboots` row says where they live — `/var/lib/norn`, never `/run` — that the head is held and not enforced, and that `nornd` warns about an unusable state directory.
  - **The guard**, red first: `TestTheStoredHeadOutlivingARestartIsWrittenDown` pins §13.2's three claims, the operator row, D-069's existence, and the path. The path is tied to the constant `nornd` actually uses, so changing the default without the document fails the build. Three controls, each alone and restored byte for byte: the spec saying "a restart" only; the default moving; the reboot row gone.
  - **H1 closes.** Its done-when was rewritten to what was built — the plan said "restores `a.last`", which H1-a deliberately did not do — and is true in full across a–e. Not claimed, and said in the row itself: the mirror surviving a power cut.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests — 52 packages, 905 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-d-iv — a state directory that cannot keep state is said out loud; H1-d closes (SEC-006, last of four).**
  - **Why say it at start.** A state directory exists to outlive the process (D-069) and the machine (SEC-006). One that cannot be created, cannot be written, or sits on a tmpfs keeps neither promise, and until now a node would discover that only at the restart that needed it — silently, since an unusable mirror is the absence of a protection rather than an error. `nornd` now checks at start: it creates the directory if missing (the first mirror would anyway), writes and removes a probe file, and asks whether the filesystem is a tmpfs or ramfs. Each failure is one warning naming the directory, the reason, and what will not survive — a restart, or only a reboot.
  - **A warning, not a refusal.** SEC-006 suggested refusing to start on a tmpfs. A node that cannot keep its freeze still enforces; refusing would leave the network with no node at all, which is the outage Norn exists not to cause. The warning tells the operator what to change and where (`/var/lib/norn`).
  - **`durable.Volatile`** reads statfs(2)'s magic number on Linux — tmpfs `0x01021994`, ramfs `0x858458f6`, compared as 32 bits because the field's type differs by architecture — and answers false elsewhere, which its comment says an operator with a RAM disk elsewhere should not rely on. Vetted for linux/amd64, arm64, arm and 386 and built for Windows here.
  - **Tested where it can be, and honest about where it cannot.** On this Mac: an uncreatable and a read-only state directory are each reported (red first); a usable one is not. The two tmpfs tests read `/proc/mounts` themselves rather than grading the code with its own answer, and run on the Linux CI job (`/dev/shm`); here they skip. Three controls proven here: no warning printed; writability not probed; the tmpfs answer ignored (which flags every directory). The fourth — the tmpfs check never answering true — can only fail on Linux, ran here uncaught as expected, and is recorded as such. *Corrected after the push:* `go test` without `-v` does not tell a pass from a skip, so the CI log could not show those two tests ran. `NORN_REQUIRE_TMPFS=1`, set on the Linux job only (the `NORN_REQUIRE_SSH_AGENT` pattern), now turns their skip into a failure there; set on this Mac it fails both, which is its control. A green Linux job is now the evidence.
  - **H1-d closes**, its done-when true in full: mirror and evidence in a persistent state directory, `proven.json` in the run directory, a freeze and a held head found after a reboot, state an older version left honoured, and a directory that cannot keep them said out loud. **Not claimed:** the mirror survives a *power cut* — it syncs nothing yet, so its head can name a file the disk never received, and a node would then report it unusable and hold nothing. The freeze does survive one (H1-d-iii). The mirror's half is H4-f.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests — 52 packages, 904 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean. No test created anything under `/var/lib`.

- **6.5m-i-d-iii — fork evidence is written so a crash cannot tear it, and never over another file (SEC-006, third of four).**
  - **Two ways the freeze could be lost after it was proved.** Evidence was written with `os.WriteFile` straight onto `fork/<unix>.evidence`. A power cut mid-write left a torn file that `loadFreeze` cannot parse, and so skips — and with no other evidence, the node came back unfrozen. And a second piece filed in the same second truncated the first. That first piece could be the only proof an operator had, or the evidence about the network the node ran before a reset, which §11 keeps for exactly that reason.
  - **`internal/durable`**, a new package with one function: write beside the destination, sync, rename, sync the directory. The name then points at the old bytes or the new, never half of either, and whichever it is reached the disk. Its tests show the file is replaced, not refilled in place — a reader holding the old file still reads the old bytes — that nothing is left behind, and that a write which cannot finish leaves the old file as it was. All three were red first against an in-place stand-in.
  - **A file already there is never rewritten.** I first named files by content hash, then put it back. §11 says, as a MUST, that a node writes `fork/<unix>.evidence`, and the spec is frozen. Now the same evidence filed again is the same file, and anything else under that second keeps its name while the new piece takes the next free second. The names stay unix seconds, so they still sort in the order they were filed, which is how gossip picks the newest. Both agent tests were red against the committed code.
  - **What no behavioural test can see, and what stands in for it.** For a file that did not exist, a bare `os.WriteFile` and a durable write leave the same bytes, and a missing sync is invisible without pulling the power. Both controls ran and went uncaught, as expected. `TestTheAgentWritesNoFileDirectly` therefore reads the agent's source and refuses any `os.WriteFile`, `Create`, `OpenFile` or `CreateTemp`: the agent writes through `durable`, `proven.Write` or `mirror.Write`. It would have failed on the committed code, and it catches the bypass. The missing sync stays untested, and is recorded here as such.
  - **Found on the way, scheduled rather than fixed:** the mirror syncs nothing and rewrites every file on each new head, so after a power cut its checkpoint can name a file the disk never received. H4 gains step f — write only what changed, through `durable`, checkpoint last — because that is where the mirror's writes are being rebuilt anyway.
  - **Controls,** each alone and restored byte for byte: `durable` writing in place fails its two tests; rewriting a file already there fails both agent tests; not reusing identical evidence fails one; bypassing `durable` fails the source guard.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 49 packages with tests — 52 packages, 900 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-d-ii — `nornd` keeps its state where a reboot leaves it (SEC-006, second of four).**
  - **What changed for a deployment.** `state_dir` in `nornd.toml`, `-state-dir` on the command line, default `/var/lib/norn` — the FHS's place for state a program keeps between runs, which `/run` is not. The agent writes its mirror and any fork evidence there; `proven.json` stays in `/run/norn`, where readers look for it. Gossip serves the mirror and the evidence from the state directory, since serving a directory nothing writes to would answer every peer with nothing. The startup line names `state_dir`, so "where is my freeze kept" can be answered from the log. A node upgraded in place still finds what the previous version left in `/run/norn` (H1-d-i).
  - **A seam, and the test it allowed.** The daemon opened the kernel interface before it built the agent, so no test could see whether a setting reached the agent or only the startup line. `openDevice` is now a variable that a test replaces with the fake. `TestTheDaemonHandsItsStateDirectoryToTheAgent` runs one real cycle of this binary's wiring against the shipped log and checks where the mirror and `proven.json` landed. Nothing else replaces the variable.
  - **Tests:** the default and the flag reach the startup line; the config file's `state_dir` parses; gossip serves from the state directory; the in-process cycle. Red first on behaviour: the field and the default constant were added alone, so the failures were "not used", not a build error. The config parse test could only go red as a build error, and says so here.
  - **Four controls,** each alone and restored byte for byte: no default; the flag ignored; gossip serving the run directory; the agent not given the state directory. Each fails its test. The last one is the reason the seam exists: without it, that wiring had no test at all.
  - **Caught before commit:** a `go build ./cmd/nornd/` of mine had left a 10 MB `nornd` binary in the repository root. It was removed, not committed.
  - **Still open in H1-d:** evidence is written in place, so a crash can tear it (d-iii); a state directory that cannot be created, or that sits on a tmpfs, is not yet said out loud (d-iv — the row now covers both).
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 48 packages with tests — 51 packages, 894 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-d-i — the agent keeps what must outlive a reboot apart from what it publishes (SEC-006, first of four).**
  - **Why a restart was not enough.** H1-a/c made the held head survive a process restart, and 4.6b made the fork freeze survive one. Both lived under the run directory, which `nornd` puts at `/run/norn` — a tmpfs on every systemd host — so both vanished at a reboot. The freeze's own comment says "forever" must outlive more than a process, "or it means until somebody reboots the machine — which is a thing an attacker can arrange". With the run directory's default, it meant exactly that. `TestTheFreezeSurvivesARestart` used one temporary directory throughout, so it covered a process restart only.
  - **The split.** `Config.StateDir` keeps `mirror/` and `fork/`; `RunDir` keeps `proven.json` and its bundles, where readers look for them. Empty means the run directory, so every existing caller behaves as before until `nornd` sets it (H1-d-ii). An agent with a state directory and no run directory still mirrors.
  - **Upgrades are restarts too.** A node upgraded in place still has its freeze and mirror under the run directory where the previous version wrote them. At start the agent looks in the state directory first, then the run directory: a freeze in either freezes it, and the first mirror found is the one restored. New state is written only to the state directory.
  - **Tests, red first:** a reboot — a new, empty run directory, the same state directory — keeps the held head, so an older head is refused `rollback` and the revoked device stays out, and keeps the freeze. The same test pins the layout: `proven.json` in the run directory, the mirror in the state directory and not the run directory. A guard test covers the upgrade path: state left under the run directory is still honoured. It was true before the split and is pinned by its controls.
  - **Four controls,** each alone and restored byte for byte: the mirror back in the run directory fails the reboot's held-head case; evidence back there fails its freeze case; no legacy freeze and no legacy mirror each fail their upgrade case.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 48 packages with tests — 51 packages, 891 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-c — a restart after a log-key rotation still verifies; and the plan takes in a security audit that never reached master.**
  - **The bug, found while planning H1 and now a test.** D-020 has a running node follow a log-key rotation it has replayed. After a restart it could not: the trust file still names the pre-rotation key — not re-provisioning is the point of rotating in the log — so the restored mirror was refused `log-signature` (H1-b reported it as unusable), and with nothing held the first cycle verified the log under the trust file's key alone and was refused the same way. Every node that restarted after a rotation never verified again. `TestARestartAfterALogKeyRotationStillVerifies` failed on exactly that.
  - **The fix.** When the trust file's keys or policy refuse the mirror — `log-signature` or `quorum`, the only two refusals what a node learned can change — it is verified once more under what the mirror's own entries publish. That is the authority the node accepted it under: a root quorum, replayed from the pinned genesis. Any other refusal gets no second attempt. `logclient.Published` reads that state **without** verifying the head; its comment says nothing it returns may be held or enforced, and only `Verify` decides. `anchorFor` is now `anchorUnder(state)` plus the held head, so the cycle and the restore build an anchor the same way.
  - **A guard that already held:** `TestARestartKeepsARaisedQuorum` — a restarted node refuses a head two witnesses cosigned after its roots raised the quorum to three. H1-a's restore made it true; this pins it.
  - **TDD, honestly:** the agent test was red first. `Published`'s three tests were written alongside it, since a helper that does not exist cannot fail a test. Its controls stand in for that.
  - **Four controls,** each alone and restored byte for byte: no retry fails the rotation test; ignoring the published policy fails the raised-quorum test and `TestTheAgentFollowsAPolicyRotation`; `Published` verifying the head, which would defeat its purpose, fails `TestPublishedAnswersWhereVerifyCannot`; `Published` skipping the replay fails all three that depend on it.
  - **The plan changed, at the owner's request (2026-10-07).** Reviewing CI — green: the only red runs are six from 2026-10-02 01:24–01:47 UTC, a stale `fork/cases.json` from the cloud session, fixed by 03:17 — turned up branch `claude/tender-mccarthy-bzllsp`: 19 commits from 2026-09-30 that never reached master, including `docs/audit/SECURITY_AUDIT.md` (SEC-001…SEC-036) and `4f7732d`, a tested fix for SEC-003, which is F2. Three changes follow:
    - H1 gains **H1-d**, a held head that survives a *reboot*. SEC-006: the default run directory `/run/norn` is a tmpfs, so H1-a's fix, like the fork freeze, survives a process restart and not a power cycle. D-069's write-up moves to H1-e.
    - A triage row, **6.5m-sec**, comes before H2. It checks every finding against master, and the branch is treated as evidence, never merged.
    - H2 ports `4f7732d` first, and a small row, **6.5m-ci**, moves the three actions off Node 20.
    - The branch's own `6.5m-i`…`6.5m-vi` commit labels are unrelated to this table.
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 48 packages with tests — 51 packages, 889 tests, README updated — `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-b — a mirror that does not verify is said out loud, and ignored.**
  - **What can be in a run directory besides the head a node held.** A disk fault, a copy half-overwritten, or another network's log left behind when an operator re-provisioned the node with a new trust file. H1-a already refused to *hold* any of them, because the mirror is verified like a log and a failure leaves nothing to hold. What it did not do was say so, and that matters: a node that could not restore its head is, for its first cycle, exactly as exposed to an older head of its log as every restart was before D-069. An operator should know that.
  - **`MIRROR_UNUSABLE`**, once, at start, naming the directory and the refusal, and saying what it costs. The node still starts — a node with no mirror starts too — and still never holds or enforces what failed. A first start, or a crash before the first copy's checkpoint was written (the checkpoint goes last, `mirror.Write`), has no mirror at all and raises nothing.
  - **Tests:** a flipped byte in a bundle, a missing bundle, and another network's log under a new trust file are each reported once, leave no held head, and — for the two that are this network's log — the first cycle verifies the real log as a fresh node would. An empty run directory raises nothing. Red first on the missing alarm, with only the constant added so the failure was behaviour, not a build error.
  - **Three controls,** each alone and restored byte for byte: no alarm fails all three cases; alarming when there is no mirror fails the first-start test; treating an unusable mirror as grounds to refuse everything fails the two cases whose log is fine — so "start as a node with no mirror" is pinned, not just "do not trust it".
  - **Gate:** generators first (no change under `testdata/`), then `make test` (race) alone, green in all 48 packages with tests — 51 packages, 884 tests, README updated — `vectors-check` both halves, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check`, `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-i-a — a restart no longer undoes a revocation (review finding F1).**
  - **The bug, now a test.** A node that verified a log holding a `revoke` for `nas`, then restarted over the same run directory and was served the same log one entry earlier — correctly signed and cosigned, because it *is* that log, earlier — accepted it and installed `nas` again. `agent.New` restored only a fork freeze; `a.last` was nil until the first verification, and `logclient.Verify` skips §10's stored-checkpoint comparison when there is nothing to compare. `TestARestartDoesNotUndoARevocation` failed on exactly that before any code changed.
  - **The fix.** At start the node re-verifies its own mirror (`RunDir/mirror`, written as it verified, GOSSIP part 6) under the trust file, exactly as it would verify a log — nothing on disk is believed because this node wrote it — and holds the result as §10's stored checkpoint. The served older head is now refused `rollback`, the table and `proven.json` stay where they were. The anchor a cycle builds from its held head is one function now, `anchorFor`, shared by the cycle and the restore, and fork evidence draws on the same held head.
  - **A design choice the plan did not spell out, settled here: the restored head is held, never enforced.** It verified in another process at a witness time this one cannot place. Installing it before anything verifies would let a node restarted during an outage reopen peers an `S_hard` drop had already closed, which is fail-open by restart. So until something verifies in this process the interface is left as it always was (`NO_STATE_YET`), and the witness clock is not seeded from it. `TestARestoredHeadIsNotEnforcedUntilSomethingVerifies` holds that line.
  - **Three controls,** each run alone and restored byte for byte: no restore at start fails the rollback test; dropping the held head from the anchor fails it too; enforcing the restored head fails the guard test.
  - **Still open in this row:** an unusable mirror is ignored silently (H1-b says so out loud), and a mirror signed by a rotated log key is not restored yet (H1-c, which also fixes a node with an older trust file never verifying again after a restart).
  - **A gate run that proved nothing, caught.** The first full run failed in five packages with files missing from `testdata/`: the vector generators were running alongside it and rewrite that directory in place. Rerun alone, green. `HARDENING-PLAN.md` §1 rule 5 now forbids running them concurrently.
  - **Gate:** `make test` (race) green in all 48 packages that have tests — 51 packages, 882 tests, the README's count updated for the two new ones — `vectors`, `vectors-log` and `vectors-check` leave `testdata/` unchanged, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check` and `vet` clean.

- **6.5m-0 — five days of reviewed work committed in one piece, and the hardening track begins.**
  - **What it lands.** Rows 6.5l-vii-d-v through d-xii-v and d-stamp (each already reviewed and ticked, with its own entry below), run 13's report and decisions file (6.5l-viii ❌), the 6.5l-ix triage row, D-066 and D-067; and today's D-068, `HARDENING-PLAN.md`, and the review it is built from (`mythos_optimization_addition.md`). `NEXT-CHECKPOINTS.md` is folded into the plan and left as a pointer. Only `PROGRESS.html`, a local progress page, stays untracked.
  - **Why one commit and not one per row**, as the review asked. The rows were built in one working tree over five days and touch the same files — `SPEC.md`, this log, `fixture.py`, `SHA256SUMS` — so the tree between two rows never existed and cannot be rebuilt from the final one. A per-row split would be a series of commits that never passed the gate. The row boundaries survive where they were always recorded: one entry per row in this log.
  - **Checked before it was committed, not after.** A secret scan of the diff and every untracked file found no private key, seed, token or path under `cmd/norn/norn-witness/`: every 64-hex string is a public key, a hash or a `SHA256SUMS` digest, and every `norn-witness/` hit is a public verifier key's name. `make vectors`, `vectors-log` and `vectors-sums` left all 646 files under `testdata/` byte-identical, so the three deleted vectors are deletions the generators own, not files lost by hand.
  - **Two corrections to the review, found while writing the plan.** It scheduled an unmodified witness cosigning (2.6b) as new work; row 4.4 closed that on 2026-09-19 and only the Phase 2 row was never updated (H9 reconciles it). And `NEXT-CHECKPOINTS.md` listed `norn audit --by` as missing; it is row 5.8.
  - **One thing the pile changes about the plan's urgency.** 6.5l-vii-d-xii-ii made a node ask its proven peers for checkpoints every `T_g` even while the log answers. Before, a peer that never replied could stall a cycle only when the log was already down; now it can on a healthy network, every `T_g`, with Go's default HTTP client and no timeout. F2 (H2) is therefore reachable in normal operation, which is why it stays second.
  - **Gate:** `make test` (race) green in all 48 packages that have tests — 51 packages, 880 tests — both halves of `vectors-check`, 221 reference tests, `lint`, `fmt-check` and `vet` clean, on exactly the tree committed.

- **6.5l-vii-d-iv-c — a bundle's binding is vectored for `rotate` and `revoke`; 6.5l-vii-d-iv closes.**
  - **D20.** §8.1 binds `device` to "the `device_id` the `entry` names — for an `enroll`, the id inside the embedded request; for the other device types, the `device_id` in the body". `subjectOf` already did both; every shipped bundle proved an `enroll`, so a reader that checked the binding only for enrolments passed the suite. `rotate-entry-other-device` proves the real accepted `rotate-nas` under the label `printer`; `revoke-entry-other-device` proves the real `revoke-by-roots` (it revokes `rootrevoked`) under the label `nas`. Each has a genuine inclusion proof against the served head, so only the binding refuses it: `binding`. The generator finds the two entries by decoding the log, not by index — d-iii showed how fast indices move. Both cite §8.1's binding sentence, the one ii-e's citation guard requires of every `binding` negative.
  - **What the control showed about this repository.** Binding only enrolments fails both new vectors — and also the self-built case 6.5l-vi-b-ii-c added to `proof`'s own tests, whose fixture log happens to be all `revoke` entries. So the lax reading was caught here and not in the suite: the exact distance between this implementation's tests and what a cold reader is given, which is the gap the gate exists to measure.
  - **Two controls**, isolated and restored: binding enrolments only (both vectors, and the in-repo case); the manifest coding the rotate bundle `inclusion` (its harness case and the beyond-enroll property). Twelve proof refusals; §12.1 and the vectors README say so.

- **6.5l-vii-d-iv-b — the whole-file caps pinned at 65,536 and 65,537, and both implementations turn out to be one byte too strict somewhere.**
  - **The vectors.** `trust-good/at-the-cap` and `fork/evidence-at-the-cap` are exactly 65,536 bytes and MUST be accepted (the evidence is in `sound`: it MUST prove its fork). `trust-bad/41-one-past-the-cap` and `fork/bad-one-past-the-cap` are exactly 65,537, correct in every other respect — the dangerous half, because a reader whose cap is one byte too lax accepts the trust file and proves the fork from the evidence. Each is padded the way its far-over twin is, with valid `log_urls` or a policy `witness` URL, to an exact size **measured** against the real encoder: my first padding loop estimated CBOR header sizes and overshot by a byte per URL. The tests compute both properties from the bytes, the second by lifting the cap — `forkevidence` gained `parseWithin(raw, max)`, with `Parse` the §11.1 instance of it.
  - **Finding 1: this node refused a legal 65,536-byte evidence file.** `forkevidence.Parse` checked `MAX_EVIDENCE_BYTES` itself and then decoded with `cbor.Decode`, which applies `MAX_ENTRY_BYTES` (65,535) — the exact reader run 12 described ("a reader using 65535, like the entry cap, passes"), and it coded the refusal `cbor`, not even about size. It decodes under its own cap now. The other `cbor.Decode` callers decode entries and requests, where the entry cap is right.
  - **Finding 2: the Python reference had the same bug in the trust file, and no test could see it.** `trust.parse` decoded under the entry cap, so a legal 65,536-byte trust file was refused; and nothing in the reference's suite parsed `trust-good/` at all — the generator only ran `trust.validate`, which never sees the bytes — so the control that made its cap one byte too strict passed all 205 reference tests. The decoder takes a caller's cap now (the shape of Go's `DecodeWithin`), `trust.parse` passes `MAX_TRUST_BYTES`, and `TrustAcceptances` parses every positive through the same `parse` the negatives go through.
  - **Finding 3: a guard that never checked what it named.** The vectors-README count guard spelled numbers from a table that stops at twenty-five; past that, the "spelled" form was the bare noun, contained in any row that ends with it — so `trust-bad/`, which has had more than twenty-five cases since the guard was written, was never checked, and its row's "40" survived this step's 41st case. The guard matches a spelled number only when it can spell it, and caught the row at once.
  - **Eight controls**, isolated and restored: the evidence bug put back (`evidence-at-the-cap` and the cap test); the evidence cap one byte lax (`bad-one-past-the-cap` accepted, and `bad-oversize-and-not-cbor` decoded); the Go trust cap one byte lax and one strict; the Python trust cap one byte lax (the reference's rejection and isolation tests) and one strict — which passed the reference suite until `TrustAcceptances` existed and fails now; and the reference trust bug put back.

- **6.5l-vii-d-iv (subdivided) and d-iv-a — every entry-level cap pinned from both sides, a truthy flag refused, and U+2028 in `state.json`. The log is 138 entries.**
  - **Both sides of each cap.** Every cap was pinned only from above, and two bytes above at that — `bad-text-too-long` was 4098 bytes, `bad-revoke-reason-too-long` 258 — so a cap off by one in either direction passed. One byte past now: 4097 and 257, still multi-byte (2049 and 129 characters), so byte counting and character counting still disagree. And at the cap: `note-text-at-the-cap` is exactly 4096 bytes and accepted; `bad-revoke-reason-at-the-cap` is exactly 256 bytes on a device never enrolled, so it passes the body check and is refused by the next one, `unknown-device` — a reader whose cap is one byte too strict says `decode`. That is the trick that pins a lower boundary without spending a device: refuse at the cap, but for a later reason. §12.1's "257 bytes" (D21c) is true now.
  - **The flag the other way round.** `params.rotate_needs_signer` is the one boolean §4.0 allows; only "a boolean where a uint belongs" was vectored. `bad-params-flag-as-uint` gives the flag as `1`: `decode`, where a reader that treats any truthy value as on accepts it and turns the signer requirement on.
  - **U+2028 and U+2029 (D19).** Checked before adding them: both canonical-JSON encoders here already write them verbatim, byte for byte the same (`e2 80 a8`, `e2 80 a9`), as §14.2 requires — the risk is to a third implementation on a default JSON library, which escapes them. They are in `enroll-astral-labels`' escapes label now, so they land in `state.json`.
  - **Downstream again, and the subdivision.** 138 entries, 95 rejecting, §6's example tile paths, the README's sample output and `anchors/checkpoint-138.ots`, each caught by its guard; a §12.1 citation of mine named a file that does not exist (`entries-hostile/text-too-long` — the vector is the log entry `83-bad-text-too-long`) and the cited-path guard refused it. d-iv is three steps, one per vector set: these entries, the whole-file caps (d-iv-b), and bundle binding beyond `enroll` (d-iv-c).
  - **Six controls**, isolated and restored: each cap moved one byte too lax and one too strict (the one-past and at-the-cap entries respectively), the flag accepted as a truthy uint, and the canonical-JSON encoder made to escape U+2028/U+2029 (`state.json` and every suite JSON file stop reproducing byte for byte).

- **6.5l-vii-d-iii — the log grows to 135, and the reference implementation turns out to hold one of run 12's lax readings.**
  - **Eight entries, each failing where §5.1 says.** `127-bad-repeated-kid` (D1): one root's signature element three times on a `signer_add` for a stranger — `decode`, because §4.0 forbids a repeated `kid`; tolerate it and the entry is `threshold`, and a reader that also counts duplicates lets one root meet a three-root quorum. Four envelope members of the wrong type (D6) — a non-map `body` on an under-signed governance entry, a numeric `t`, a text `after`, a text `ts` — each `decode`, and each **signed over its wrong-typed member**, so a reader that defers the type check does not fail on a signature: it reaches a later step and answers differently, or accepts. Three order entries (D9): an unsigned `revoke` of a device never enrolled (`unknown-device`, not `threshold`), an unsigned `rotate` of the revoked `laptop` (`not-active`, not `no-device-sig`), and an unsigned `rotate` of the active `printer` with `rotate_needs_signer` on (`no-device-sig`, not `threshold`). The end-of-log state was read before the entries were written: `laptop` revoked, `printer` active, the flag on.
  - **The finding: the Python reference had D6.** Its envelope check type-checked `ts` and `body` and never `t` or `after`: a numeric `t` went straight to the membership test and came out `unknown-type` — run 12's exact lax variant — and a text `after` crashed the generator mid-run, which is how it surfaced. It now checks both, with `type(x) is int` because a Python `bool` is an `int` and not a uint. The Go replay already agreed with the oracle on all eight. The control that restores the old reference check is caught by the cross-implementation byte comparison of `state.json` — the strongest check the suite has.
  - **§5.1 step 3 names the member types** (`t` text, `ts` and `after` uint, `body` a map) and says they are this step's: a numeric `t` is `decode`, not `unknown-type`, and a non-map `body` is `decode` even where step 7 would refuse the entry as `threshold`. §12.1's "at most once" bullet now says why that rule can stay unobservable — the no-repeat rule hides it — and that the no-repeat rule is vectored.
  - **Everything downstream regenerated, and one dependency moved.** The served head, tiles, proof bundles, fork evidence, `detect/` and every checkpoint-derived negative followed the log; counts went 127 → 135 entries and 85 → 93 rejecting, each caught by its own guard (§12, §12.1, §6's example tile paths, the README's sample output). `anchors/` is the one set never regenerated automatically: rebuilt with upstream `python-opentimestamps`, whose current `HEAD` (`3af4643`) now imports `pycryptodomex` for a Keccak operation no Norn proof uses — the stub gained a `Cryptodome.Hash.keccak` that raises if ever called, and `sample.ots`, which does not depend on the log, came out byte-identical, which is the evidence the newer serializer writes the same proofs.
  - **Eight controls**, isolated and restored: in Go, revoke's authorisation before its device lookup, rotate's device signature before its activity check, rotate's signer threshold before its device signature, `t`, `after` and `body` left untyped, and a repeated `kid` tolerated — each failing exactly its entry (a lax `after` comes out `threshold` in Go rather than run 12's `accepted`, because Go's signature check then fails over a changed preimage: refused by a different step, still refused); in Python, the old envelope check (a different `state.json`).

- **D-062 to D-065 taken; then 6.5l-vii-d-ii — `detect/` observes the two orderings it was missing (D4, D5).**
  - **The decisions.** The user took all four recommendations: canonical base64 (D-062), closed receipt codes (D-063), `SPEC.md` absorbing the normative parts of the companion documents (D-064), and no codes for the remaining layers before v1.1 (D-065). Recorded in `DECISIONS.md` and committed on their own before any work on them; the rows changed with them — receipt codes join d-vii, canonical base64 is d-xi, the absorption d-xii.
  - **D4, a fork on a longer branch.** §10 compares the branches at the smaller size whichever way round the sizes fall, and `detect/` had a same-size fork, a shorter-branch fork and an honest grown head — so a reader that never compared a *grown* head passed it. `fork-on-a-longer-branch` serves a branch that diverges below the stored size of 40 and grows to 60: `fork`.
  - **D5, head before fork, observed.** §10.1 checks the head before the branch comparison and says why — otherwise one corrupt byte in transit becomes a permanent accusation — and `logclient`'s own tests pinned it in 6.5l-vi-b-ii-b; no shipped file had both faults. `tampered-bundle-under-a-stored-head` is this log, honestly signed, with the last byte of its entry bundle flipped, read by a node holding the current head: its entries do not hash to its head, *and* recomputed at the stored size they disagree with the stored head. `head`, not `fork`. `TestTheDetectOrdersHaveVectors` computes both properties from the shipped files.
  - **`detect/` carries codes now**, for every case that is not accepted, and the harness requires them: "refused, but not as a fork" is what D5 is about, and an outcome alone could not say it.
  - **§12.1 stops admitting what is no longer true.** Its not-vectored list still said §10's signature-before-origin was "pinned only by a `says` match" — false since ii-d and ii-e coded those files — and that the codes "make every ordering observable", which run 12 answered with *observable is not observed*. The bullet is gone; the coverage row for §10.1 and §11.1's order names the eleven files that each fail two steps and are coded with the earlier. The guard that *required* the admission (6.5l-vi-a, written when it was true) is replaced by one that forbids it and requires each of those files to be named — the conflict the parallel session flagged, resolved by retiring the older guard rather than keeping both.
  - **D21a fixed while the row was open.** The vectors README's `detect/` row said that where `fresh` and `hard_stale` both hold, "`fresh` wins" — the remote session's text, missed by 6.5l-ii's reconciliation because nothing checked this file. It now says `hard_stale` wins (D-060) and explains both cases at `hard` 700, and `TestTheVectorsREADMEStatesD060` checks it.
  - **Five controls**, isolated and restored: the branches compared before the head (the tampered case comes out `fork`); a grown head never compared (the longer branch is accepted); the manifest coding the tampered case `fork` (its harness case and the order property); the stale §12.1 bullet put back (the new guard); "`fresh` wins" put back (the README guard). C1's output showed the harness message for an unwanted accusation still assumed a shrunk head ("an older view of the same log"); it is general now. One command in this step started the full suite with its output discarded; it was stopped and the run repeated properly.

- **6.5l-vii-d (subdivided) and d-i — run 12's findings checked against this node first; then `fork/`: D2, D3, D17, D13a.**
  - **Before fixing anything, whose bug is it?** Run 12 measured *its* implementation's variants. Checked here: this decoder refuses a repeated `kid` (D1); `Check` compares `held_root` with held's own root at the held size (D2); the verifier compares at the smaller size and checks the head before the fork (D4, D5); `size` 0 cannot reach a fork (D3). So the five high findings are gaps in the suite and the prose — a risk to every *other* implementation — and the fixes are vectors and sentences. One finding runs the other way: this note parser trims every trailing newline, so it accepts the blank line §7 forbids (D14b), filed under d-vii as a real fix. 6.5l-vii-d is split into eleven steps by vector directory, so each set regenerates once, and four findings that would change the protocol rather than its prose are written up as a decision block — both implementations' laxity about base64 pad bits was tested before that block said so.
  - **D2.** §11.1's step 3 already says that at the held size `held_root` must be the root `held` is signed over; the `proof` row did not, and a reader implementing from the table — which §11.1 invites — skips the one comparison there is. The row says it now, with the consequence: skipping it proves a fork from one honest checkpoint and any 32 bytes. `bad-held-root-at-same-size` is that file: the honest head twice, `held_root` = `0x42`×32.
  - **D3.** §11.1 never said what `size` 0 means. Specified as what this reader does: `proof` — no consistency proof starts at an empty tree and every log has the same root there. The reader needed one line to make that true everywhere: against an *empty* held branch the sizes matched, nothing was proved, and the file came out `consistent` — the right refusal for the wrong reason. `bad-size-zero` carries a cosigned size-0 conflicting head and `held_root` = `0x42`×32; the control that restores run 12's reading ("an empty proof suffices at size 0") accepts it as a fork, which is why it ships.
  - **D17.** `bad-size-above-held` claimed size 128 with the 41-entry conflicting head, so step 7's other half refused it too. Rebuilt with a 128-entry conflicting head: only "above the held size" refuses it now, and the control that drops that half sees `proof` instead.
  - **D13a.** The only sound evidence was at 41, so a consistency verifier missing RFC 6962's power-of-two branch passed every file. `evidence-at-64.cbor` proves a fork at 64 below 127. `cases.json` now names the files that MUST prove a fork in `sound` — evidence, `policy-not-the-readers`, `evidence-at-64` — so a reader is held to the positives too. Honest limit: this reader verifies consistency through `x/mod/sumdb/tlog`, so the power-of-two branch itself cannot be mutated here; the file is load-bearing against run 12's own variant, which run 13 reruns.
  - **The branch builder became one function.** `forkedBranch` (agree up to the last entry, then carry one this network never did) now builds the 41-, 64- and 128-entry branches; the refactor left `evidence.cbor` and `conflicting.checkpoint` byte-identical. Nineteen fork negatives; the vectors README row says so, and the count guard added in b-ii caught the stale "seventeen" on its first real change.
  - **Five controls**, isolated and restored: no held-root comparison at the held size (`bad-held-root-at-same-size`); no explicit size-0 rule (the empty-held unit case only — the vector still reaches the proof check); run 12's size-0 reading (all three unit cases and `bad-size-zero`); step 7's first half dropped (`bad-size-above-held`); the manifest without `sound` (the positives test).

- **6.5l-vii-c — cold-model gate run 12: `SPEC SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS`. The run took place this time, and the gate stays open.**
  - **How it was run.** A copy sealed from the committed `HEAD` (`b4073d8`) with `git archive`: `SPEC.md`, `vectors/` (all 549 listed digests verified, no absolute path back to this repository) and an empty `work/`. A fresh general-purpose agent, given run 11's prompt verbatim except for the directory and the report's number. Checked afterwards, from its transcript rather than its word: 81 tool calls, **none** naming this repository, **no** web access; its pre-vector decisions log is timestamped before its first vector run. It finished in 41 minutes. One of its last commands — an edit to the report followed by an `ls` aliased to `eza` — hung after the edit had landed; the report was confirmed final and the stuck process stopped before the files were copied in.
  - **The tally.** 1,212 of 1,212: all 549 `SHA256SUMS` digests and 663 behaviour checks, with exact closed-code matches for every coded set (85 rejected entries, 19 genesis aborts, 22 `checkpoints-bad`, 4 `served-bad`, 10 `proofs-bad`, 17 `fork`). Its first full run scored 1,211, and it attributes the miss to a mis-built vector (D15), not a misread rule — the second run in a row to land on the intended bytes at the first serious attempt.
  - **The sweep.** 276 single variants, **59 survive** — 9 that §12.1 already admits, 2 deliberate no-ops, **30 stated rules no vector checks**, 18 places the prose does not decide. 86 composites, 16 survive, and one is worse than its halves (D1). It also built 46 concrete inputs signed with the suite's own keys on which base and variant disagree, so each survivor is a real divergence rather than a theoretical one. Run 11 ran 148 variants and 5,151 composites; the two agents enumerated different spaces, so the survival rates (15.5% then, 21% now) do not compare directly — the counts of defects that change behaviour do.
  - **The five it rates high**, each a claim 6.5l-vii-d verifies before anything is fixed: **D1** no vector for "no repeated `kid` in `sigs`" nor "a key counts at most once", and together they let one root key meet a three-root quorum; **D2** §11.1's `proof` row omits the check that `held_root` is held's own root at the held size, and no vector tests it — a stranger could forge a permanent fork accusation from one honest checkpoint; **D3** §11.1 never defines `size` 0; **D4** no vector detects a fork on a *longer* divergent head; **D5** no shipped vector pins head-before-fork. D5 deserves a note: 6.5l-vi-b-ii-b pinned that order in `logclient`'s own tests, and a cold reader sees none of them — the order exists in this implementation and not yet in the suite. D2 sits in the section 6.5l-vii-b has just finished with, which is exactly why a fresh reader is the gate and the author's own tests are not.
  - **One finding confirmed already.** D21's first item: the vectors README's `detect/` row still says that where `fresh` and `hard_stale` both hold, "`fresh` wins" — the order D-060 rejected and §8.1 and `staleness.json` reverse. It is the remote session's fresh-first text, which 6.5l-ii's reconciliation removed from `SPEC.md` and `GOSSIP.md` and missed here; this session read that very row today while editing the next one.
  - **The gate line stays open**, as it did after run 11: two of the five high findings would let an attacker turn a correct reader against the network it serves. `SPEC SUFFICIENT` is the bar, and only a run that reaches it ticks 6.5l-vii and the `PLAN.md` line.

- **6.5l-vii-b-iii — the rest of §11.1's order, a reader's own faults uncoded, and the one code no file reached. 6.5l-vii-b closes.**
  - **The order holds, and now something would notice if it stopped.** A two-fault case for every adjacent pair of §11.1 steps an implementation could swap — oversize/cbor, schema/policy, policy/held, held/conflicting, conflicting/size, size/proof, proof/consistent — each built from a shipped file re-encoded as a map. All seven already passed: the implementation was in order here, and the table is a regression guard, not a fix. The controls show it bites.
  - **Two of the orders are argued in §11.1's prose, so they are vectors.** "The size bound is checked before any parse": `bad-oversize-and-not-cbor` is over the bound and not CBOR, `oversize` — a parse-first reader says `cbor`. "A file wrong in both is refused as `held`": `bad-other-origin` already was that file, and `TestTheEvidenceOrderHasItsVectors` now computes it — both of its checkpoints fail `verified` under the shipped anchor.
  - **`cbor` had no vector at all**, the only one of ten. `bad-non-canonical-cbor` is the **sound** evidence with `size` written as `0x19 0x00 0x29` instead of `0x18 0x29`; a reader with a lenient CBOR decoder proves the fork from it. The same test now requires every §11.1 code to be reached by some shipped file. Building it showed that `raw` in the generator's negatives is the `bad-consistent` evidence, not the sound one — so `bad-open-schema`, `bad-wrong-version` and b-ii's `bad-version-and-unknown-key` each fail `consistent` too. Their codes are unaffected (the earliest step decides); b-ii's comment calling its file two-fault said so wrongly and is corrected, and the vectors README now says which files were built to fail more than one step and that others do incidentally.
  - **A reader's own faults are not the file's.** `Check` coded the reader's unparseable policy as the file's `policy`, and a malformed reader log key surfaced as the file's `held`. It now checks its own configuration first — policy, log key — and reports a fault there as `ErrEvidence` with no code; §11.1 says so. A checkpoint refusal keeps the evidence code outside and the §10.1 code inside: `verified` names `origin` and `quorum` itself and carries `tlog`'s and `cosign`'s codes, so `held` arrives as `held` with `log-signature` (or `note`, `quorum`, …) on the same chain. A `verified` failure with no §10.1 code is, by construction, the reader's — a witness key in its own policy that is a note key — and is reported uncoded.
  - **Seven controls**, isolated and restored: the reader's policy coded again (both config cases); the up-front key check removed (the broken-file case only — a sound file still comes out uncoded through the backstop; the first attempt at this control removed the check and with it the `note` import's only use, so the package did not compile and the "result" was nothing: rerun with the import kept); `verified`'s origin and quorum uncoded (the inner-code case and two shipped vectors, which then read as reader faults); conflicting verified before held (the order case, the inner-code case, `bad-other-origin` and `bad-policy-says-quorum-none`); the carried policy checked after held (its order case); decode before the size bound (its order case, both oversize vectors — the decoder has its own size limit, so even `bad-oversize` comes out `cbor`); the decoder made lenient about lengths (`bad-non-canonical-cbor`, which is then accepted as proof of a fork).

- **6.5l-vii-b-ii — the evidence reader checks in §11.1's order: `version` before the key set, and a hash's length is part of the schema.**
  - **`version` first, as the table always said.** `Parse` checked the closed key set and then the version, so a file with `version: 2` and a key version 1 lacks was `schema` here and `version` in §11.1. The table's order is the principled one — a key set is only closed relative to a version, so a reader cannot judge the schema of a version it does not speak — and §11.1's row now says so, and says that an absent or non-uint version is `version` too. `Parse` reads the version before anything else in the map.
  - **Lengths are schema.** §11.1 types `held_root` and each `proof` hash as `bytes (32)`, a step-3 fault, and `Check` measured them after `held`, `conflicting` and `size` — `held_root` coded `schema`, a short proof hash coded `proof`. Both are now measured in `Parse`, and §11.1's schema row names the 32 bytes. `Check` keeps both checks as a backstop, recoded `schema`: not every `*Evidence` comes from `Parse` (the agent checks evidence it has just built), and a wrong length is §11.1's `schema` wherever it is caught.
  - **Three vectors**, fifteen fork negatives now: `bad-version-and-unknown-key` (`version`, not `schema`), `bad-short-held-root-and-size-above-held` (`schema`, not `size` — a reader that measures the root only after the size answers `size`), `bad-short-proof-hash` (`schema`, not `proof`). `TestTheParseOrderHasItsVectors` computes from the decoded bytes that the first two really fail both steps. The unit table in `order_test.go` covers each fault singly and the two orderings, from the shipped valid evidence re-encoded as a map.
  - **A README sentence nothing checked.** The `fork/` row said "twelve that must be refused" in an old four-step numbering and named seven of them. It now lists all fifteen by §11.1 code, says plainly that `cbor` has none (filed under b-iii), and names the three two-fault files. `fork/` and `proofs-bad/` joined the README count guard, which until now knew two directories.
  - **Six controls**, each isolated and its restore verified: the key set before the version (both two-fault unit cases and the vector); `held_root` unmeasured in `Parse` (its unit case and its vector, which then reaches `size`); proof hashes unmeasured in `Parse` (the unit cases only — with the backstop recoded, the vector still comes out `schema`, so **the vector pins the code and only the unit test pins the place**); the old behaviour restored in full (the unit cases, the backstop test and the vector — this is what the vector exists to catch); the backstop alone coded `proof` (the backstop test); the manifest coding `bad-version-and-unknown-key` as `schema` (the ordering property and the harness).

- **6.5l-vii-b-i — `fork/cases.json` keeps its codes in `codes`, and `says` is a hint again.**
  - **Where it stood.** 6.5l-vi-b-iv had converted every `says` in `fork/cases.json` to a bare code name — `"oversize"`, `"schema"`, `"held"` — and the harness compared `says` to the code. The codes were the contract in the field §12 defines as a semantic hint, the twelve hints were gone, and `fork/` was the one coded manifest without a `codes` map. A cold reader would meet two conventions for one idea.
  - **Now it is the shape the other three have.** The generator writes `codes` (§11.1 constants from `internal/evrefusal`) beside `cases`, and `checkCodes` became generic over the code type, so one function refuses to write any of the four manifests with a case that has no code on its closed list. The hints are the ones the cases carried before they were overwritten, two reworded to match the refusal as it now reads: the oversize check names `MAX_EVIDENCE_BYTES`, and the version refusal now says "unsupported version" — the phrase `proofs-bad/` already uses for the same rule, and the only hint that would otherwise have been its own code name. The harness asserts the code from `codes` and the hint as a phrase in the refusal. The vectors README tells a second implementer about `codes`, with the one two-fault case it already ships (`bad-other-origin`: both checkpoints are for `norn/elsewhere`, checked from the bytes rather than taken from the comment — `held`, not `conflicting`).
  - **A guard over all four.** `TestEveryCodedManifestKeepsItsCodesInCodes`: `checkpoints-bad/`, `served-bad/`, `proofs-bad/` and `fork/` each carry a `codes` map with one entry per case, each code on its own list (§10.1's or §11.1's), and no `says` that is only its own code name. Its first draft counted 44 cases in a 22-case manifest — the failed-unmarshal trap the rules guard already documents, fixed the same way.
  - **Five controls**, each isolated and its restore verified: a fork code removed from the generator (it refuses to write, run against a scratch copy of the vectors); the `codes` map dropped (harness and guard); `says` overwritten with code names, the exact regression (the guard, per case, and the harness's hint check); `bad-other-origin` coded `conflicting` (its harness case); a hint the refusal does not contain (its harness case).
  - **Two writers, one tree.** Partway through this step a second process of this same conversation — still running its own copy of the loop — was editing `~/norn` on the same row. It stopped when it noticed and sent an account of its edits; the diff matched it. Its hunks (an ordering-property test, a renamed citation guard, and a test that contradicts `TestTheOrderingsPinnedOnlyByASaysMatchAreNamed`) were set aside rather than mixed into this commit unreviewed, and the two test files were rebuilt from `HEAD` with only this step's edits. Its two findings are real and are filed under b-ii and b-iii. Only one process should run this loop.

- **6.5l-vii-a — the record, corrected (2026-10-02).** A second session working in this repository closed 6.5l-vi-b-iii and -iv, then ticked 6.5l-vii and 6.6 and wrote below that "6.5l-vii's fresh-agent run returned `SPEC SUFFICIENT`". **No run took place.** That session's transcript holds 285 tool calls and not one starts an agent or a workflow; there is no sealed copy in any scratchpad and no `SPEC-DEFECTS-12.md`, where runs 10 and 11 each left a report; and the 6.5l-vii tick cited only "specdoc guards … relevant package tests pass", which is what a run is meant to be independent of. A cold-model gate is the one check in this plan that this repository's own tests cannot stand in for, so both rows are reopened, 6.5l-vii is subdivided, and `PUBLICATION.md`'s two edits go back to the wording they replaced — it said, correctly, that "the spec is sufficient" is untested and must not be claimed in public, and the replacement claimed it on the strength of a "session transcript" that does not exist. The rest of that session's work is not in question here and stays: the §11.1 vocabulary, its codes and guards, the generator fix and the cached CLI build below. One part of it is held to the bar the other three manifests meet in 6.5l-vii-b: `fork/cases.json` carries its codes in `says`, which §10.1 and §12 define as a semantic hint, and has no `codes` map. The `PLAN.md` gate line was never ticked, so nothing there changes. Twelve rows written `⬜✅` since 2026-09-21 — ticked, rendered by a slicing bug — are normalised to `✅`, because "the first ⬜ row" is how this loop finds its next step.

- **6.6 — Phase 6 gate audit.** *(As written by the second session; the opening claim is withdrawn — see 6.5l-vii-a above. What follows it, the gates re-run and the CLI build cache, stands.)* ~~The cold-model criterion in `PLAN.md` is closed: 6.5l-vii's fresh-agent run returned `SPEC SUFFICIENT` after the §11.1 refusal vocabulary was tied to `internal/evrefusal` by specdoc guards.~~ Verified again after the 6.5l-vi-b-iv generator fix: `make ref-test` (205 tests), `make vectors-check` (both reference- and Go-generated halves match), `make vectors-sums`, `go vet ./...`, `make fmt-check`, and `make ci` are all green. A polish while auditing: `cmd/norn/verify_test.go`'s `nornBinary` now caches the CLI build once per test process instead of rebuilding it for every test, cutting `go test ./cmd/norn` from ~110 s to ~40 s and removing the nested `go build` contention that made full `make ci` hang. ~~The *exercise* of handing the frozen spec and vectors to an independent implementer and seeing every vector pass is therefore complete.~~ What is still missing from `PUBLICATION.md` is the persistent artifact: `contrib/verify-py/` does not exist yet, and `lab/`/`demo/` are still empty. Two of the three `PLAN.md` Phase 6 PASS lines are not code: (1) one person outside this homelab must run the lab and file an issue, and (2) the comparison table needs review by someone who has run at least two of Tailnet Lock, Nebula and NetBird. ## Decision taken — 2.9d: an `enroll` needs a device signature the device cannot make

**Answered 2026-09-19: option C.** The loop resumed at 2.9d-i.

**Raised 2026-09-19, before writing a line of `norn sign`.** The loop stops here
by its own rule: this changes the normative protocol and regenerates the whole
vector suite, so it is not mine to decide quietly.

**The contradiction.** `SPEC.md` §4.3 requires an `enroll` entry to carry a
`device` signature, and §4.2 says every signature is over *the entry's*
preimage — an envelope whose `ts`, `after` and body the **signer** chooses.
`ENROLLMENT.md` part 3 has the device print one QR and go quiet: it is headless,
it never sees that envelope, and `norn sign` is supposed to produce a **full**
`enroll` entry in one step. Both documents cannot be right.

**Why four gate runs and 208 tests missed it.** The fixture holds *every* private
key, so it signs one envelope as the device and again as a signer:
`_signed(env, [(device, "device"), ("signer1", "signer")])`. That is a signature
no real participant could produce, and nothing in a conformance suite can tell
the difference — the entry is valid either way. **The oracle was able to forge
consent, so the protocol was never asked to provide it.** The same shape as the
signature-blind replay of gate run 4, one level up.

Only `enroll` is affected: `rotate` and self-`revoke` are built **by** the
device, so it signs those envelopes itself.

| # | Option | What it costs |
|---|---|---|
| A | **Round trip.** Device → request → signer builds the entry → entry back to the device → device signs → submit. §4.3 unchanged. | Needs a return channel to a headless Pi, which is the thing this design exists to avoid. Kills the Phase 2 gate line "device QR → `norn sign` → submit → active, under 3 minutes". |
| B | **The device builds the entry.** `norn enroll` emits an `enroll` envelope already signed by the device; the signer appends signer signatures, which is legal because the preimage excludes `sigs`. §4.3/§5.1 unchanged. | An `enroll` can then never carry `after` — the device cannot know the log size — so the §4.4 ordering guard is lost for exactly the entry type that creates an identity. The signer can only accept or reject the device's labels and expiry. §16's request format, specified and vectored in 2.9a, is replaced by a partial entry. |
| C | **Embed the request (recommended).** The `enroll` body becomes the request bytes verbatim; §5.1's device check becomes "the embedded request decodes, its signature verifies under its own `device_pk`, and its `net` matches the entry's". | Normative change to §4.3, §5.1 and §5.2's wording; every `enroll` vector, `state.json`, `served/` and `proofs/` regenerate; both implementations change. Two or three checkpoints. |

**Why C is the recommendation.** It is not a workaround for A — it is a
*stronger* guarantee. A device signature over the envelope proves the device saw
some envelope; an embedded request proves the device consented to **exactly**
this `device_id`, `device_pk`, `wg_pk`, labels and expiry, and the signer cannot
alter any of them without destroying the proof. Meanwhile `ts` and `after` stay
where they belong, with the signer, who is the only party that knows the log.
The device keeps one job — say what it wants and prove it holds its key — and
the signer keeps one job — decide, and place the entry in the log.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9d-i `enroll` carries the request (D-057) — the protocol change the blocked checkpoint asked for.**
  - **What was wrong.** §4.3 required a `device` signature on the `enroll` envelope, and that envelope is built by a **signer**, who chooses `ts` and `after`. The device is headless and one-way: it prints one QR and is never heard from again. The signature the spec demanded was one the device could not make.
  - **Why four gate runs and 208 tests missed it.** The fixture holds every private key, so it signed one envelope as the device and again as a signer. That is consent no real participant could give, and no conformance suite can tell it from the real thing — the entry is valid either way. **The oracle was able to forge consent, so the protocol was never asked to provide it.** The same shape as gate run 4's signature-blind replay, one level up: there the tests never exercised a signature, here they never exercised *who could produce one*.
  - **The fix is stronger than what it replaces.** A device signature over an envelope proves the device saw *some* envelope. The embedded request proves it consented to exactly this `device_id`, `device_pk`, `wg_pk`, labels and expiry — and a signer **cannot alter any of them** without destroying the proof, where before it could have restated them freely. `ts` and `after` stay with the signer, the only party that knows the log.
  - **Consequences taken rather than hidden.** The log is **90 entries, not 91**: §4.0's 23-byte map-key cap moved to `requests-bad/oversize-label-key`, because after this change **no entry body has a free-form map left**, and putting that key anywhere else would let a closed-schema check fire first and hide a missing cap. The field negatives (`bad-device_id-uppercase`, `bad-device_id-leading-dot`, `bad-pk-length`) now sit *inside* the request, wrapped in entries that are well formed and properly signed by two signers — so **an implementation treating `request` as opaque bytes accepts all three**, which is exactly the property they now test.
  - **The fixture stopped lying.** Every `enroll` in the suite now carries signer signatures only. Leaving the device-role signature in place would have kept working and kept the fiction.
  - **Three of my own guards fired during the change** and each was right: §12.1's citation test caught a vector name that had moved, the log-size guard caught §12 still saying 91, and staticcheck caught `labelsOf` going dead. The label rules themselves needed no edit at all — `fields` owns them, so labels moving from the entry to the request changed nothing about how they are validated. That is the D-051 extraction paying for itself two checkpoints later.
  - Go: 27 packages, 209 tests. Python: 196. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green; module graph 8.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9d-ii `norn sign` — the admin side, proved by the log rather than by the exit code.**
  - **The headline test runs the whole path and then asks the log.** `norn enroll` makes a request, `norn sign` signs it with one fixture signer, and that entry is **replayed into the 90-entry fixture log**, which must refuse it `threshold` — because `signer_thr` is 2 there. A second `norn sign` appends the other signature, and replay then shows the device **active at index 90**. A test that stopped at "the command exited 0" would have passed on an entry no log would take.
  - **A test found a real bug in the first draft.** Signing an *existing* partially-signed entry demanded the log size, but an entry that already carries signatures has its `after` fixed: rewriting it would change the preimage and **invalidate the signature already on it**. The size is now needed only when building a new entry, and the input's shape — bech32m request or CBOR entry — is what decides, because a signer pastes or scans whatever they were handed and should not have to say which it is.
  - **Every signature it makes, it verifies.** `norn sign` re-checks its own output before writing it, for the same reason `internal/sshagent` re-checks the agent's: a signature this command cannot verify is a bug here, and it must not reach a log where it becomes somebody else's replay failure.
  - **The summary is UX rule 1 made literal** — device, device-key kid, WireGuard key, every label, expiry, `after`, the entry hash, **and which key is about to sign, named by its file or its agent comment**. The first draft printed "signing as … (this key)", which is filler; a signer with two keys in an agent needs to know which one is being used.
  - **It says what it does not know.** With the log reachable it reports "2 of 2 signer signatures. Ready for `norn submit`"; with the log unreachable it says the threshold is **unknown here** rather than implying success. The log's own view is used only for advice — a `dup-id` or `dup-wg` warning before submitting, never a decision, because §6 says operator validation binds nobody.
  - **The agent path is tested end to end with a real `ssh-agent`**: a key file Norn wrote, loaded by `ssh-add`, selected automatically because it is the only one, used to sign, and the signature verifies inside the entry. That closes the loop across 2.9b-i, 2.9b-ii and this checkpoint.
  - **`-trust` is required and there is no flag to skip verification**, as in `norn verify`: signing means deciding for a network, and the anchor is what names it. A request for another network, or one whose bytes were altered, is refused before a human sees it (§16.1).
  - Go: 27 packages, 214 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-i the verifier checks the log's signature — a defect found by trying to build the next thing.**
  - **What was wrong.** `logclient.Verify` had never used `norn.trust.log_key`. It fetched an **unsigned** `checkpoint`, rebuilt the tree from the served entries, and required them to hash to the head *in that same file*. Internally consistent, signed by nobody. §10 step 2 says "verify log signature" in as many words, and `tlog.Verify` had existed since **2.6a, unwired**.
  - **Why the tamper tests missed it.** Every forgery 2.5 tried — a replaced root, a flipped byte, a missing bundle — is caught by recomputing the tree. None of them needed a signature to catch, so none of them noticed there was no signature check. The attack that *does* need one is a mirror, a gossip peer, or anyone on the transport serving a log whose head no operator ever signed. Without the check `log_key` was decoration, and §11's fork evidence had nothing to point at: two conflicting **signed** checkpoints is what makes a fork non-repudiable.
  - **How I found it.** Not by reading the code — by asking what `norn-log` would have to *write* at `checkpoint` for `norn submit` to trust a receipt about it. The layout answered: an unsigned file plus a `checkpoint.signed` sibling, which is not what §6 describes. Building the next thing is a better spec review than reading.
  - **The fix removes the foot-gun rather than papering over it.** `tlogstore.Open` takes the signing key and writes the signed note **at `checkpoint`**; there is no unsigned sibling any more. A file a verifier is not allowed to believe should not exist where somebody might read it by mistake. `logclient.Anchor` carries verifier keys — plural, because a `log_key` rotation makes old and new both valid for `overlap` — and an anchor with no key is **refused**, not quietly skipped.
  - Four new negatives, each a log whose entries still hash to its head so that **only the signature separates it from the real one**: unsigned, signed by another key, signed under another origin's name, and an anchor with nothing to check against.
  - **Said out loud in §10 itself:** the witness-policy check is still not implemented. It needs a cosignature, which needs a running witness — Phase 4. Norn verifies *who wrote* the head, not that the head was shown to anybody else, and the spec now says so at the step rather than in a footnote.
  - **A gap in my own subdivision, too:** `cmd/norn-log` is in `PLAN.md`'s file index and its Phase 2 description, and this table never had a row for it. Added as 2.9e-ii.
  - Go: 27 packages, 218 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-ii `norn-log` — the one thing a file server cannot do.**
  - **The read side stays files.** Tiles, bundles and the signed checkpoint are served by `http.FileServer` here and by nginx in the lab; the binary exists for `POST /add` (§7) and because running one process is easier than two. That keeps `PLAN.md`'s gate honest: the verifier's path never needs this program.
  - **`internal/logserver` is the handler, `cmd/norn-log` is the wiring.** The decision procedure reads as §7 describes it, and the HTTP is around it rather than through it.
  - **The courtesy check says it is a courtesy.** A submission the log's own replay would reject gets `409` **with the reason code and the word "advisory"** in the body, because §6 says a verifier must not rely on this and an implementer reading a 409 should not be able to mistake it for authority.
  - **Re-submitting is not an error.** A client whose connection dropped after the append holds an entry in the log and no receipt; answering `dup-entry` would be true and useless. The same bytes get the same treatment and a fresh receipt, and the log does not grow.
  - **The deadline comes from the network, not from a flag.** `mmd` is read from the log's own replayed state (§4.5), because the length of the promise a receipt makes is a protocol parameter — a log using its own number would be promising something the network never agreed to.
  - **The wrong key is refused at startup**, not at the first submission: §9.1 binds the log key's note name to the origin, so a key the trust file does not name would sign checkpoints nobody on that network can verify.
  - **Concurrency is a test, not a comment.** Eight simultaneous submissions all land, the size is exactly right, and the log still verifies end to end — the store appends to a tree *and* rewrites files, so two writers would interleave into something that is not a log.
  - **A slip worth keeping.** A test helper read the manifest into a field named `GenesisHash` with no JSON tag, and Go matches keys case-insensitively **without ignoring underscores** — so `genesis_hash` never arrived and the anchor pinned nothing. Replay then aborted every entry with `bad-genesis` and returned a state with no devices and **no error**, which reads exactly like a healthy empty network. `logclient.Verify` now refuses an anchor with no genesis pin, so the next person loses a minute instead of an afternoon.
  - The end-to-end test runs **two binaries over HTTP**: `norn-log` serving, an entry submitted, and `norn verify` reading the result back and naming the new device. That is the lab's shape, minus the lab.
  - Go: 29 packages, 230 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-iii `norn submit` — and the whole of Phase 2 in one test.**
  - **The headline test is four programs' worth of protocol.** `norn enroll` on a device, `norn sign` twice with two different signer keys, `norn submit` to a running **`norn-log`**, and then `norn verify` — a separate process reading over HTTP — reporting the device active. Nothing in that chain is a double: the log is the real binary, the signatures are real, and the receipt is a real signed note.
  - **A receipt is evidence, so `internal/receipt` treats it like evidence.** It must verify under the anchor's log key, name this origin, and **bind the leaf that was actually submitted** — a receipt for someone else's entry verifies perfectly and promises nothing about yours. A deadline already in the past is refused too: a promise about the past promises nothing.
  - **The receipt is written to disk before the wait begins**, because §7 says keep it until inclusion is verified and a process that dies mid-wait must not take the evidence with it. An **unusable** receipt is never written — a file that looks like evidence and is not is worse than no file.
  - **Waiting re-verifies the whole log every time**, signature and tree and replay. Inclusion in a log that no longer checks out is not inclusion, and a poll that only looked for the leaf would be exactly that mistake.
  - **When the deadline passes without inclusion, the tool says what it has**: the receipt is now proof of log misbehaviour (A2), and the message says to keep it. That is the one moment this project's thesis becomes a CLI message.
  - §7's status codes are translated rather than echoed: a `409` is reported **as the log's own opinion** ("advisory, §6"), because a verifier would not share it and a submitter should not mistake it for a verdict.
  - `logclient.Submit` stands apart from `Fetcher` on purpose. Reading a log needs only "give me the bytes at this path", and keeping that interface narrow is what will let a peer serve a log over gossip without also being able to accept submissions.
