  - **Every length in the file is somebody else's number**, because a `.ots` arrives from a calendar over the network: depth, branch count, operand size and calendar URI are all bounded, and five malformed files are refused rather than parsed — including trailing bytes glued onto a valid proof.
  - **An architecture test for D-009's own words.** "Never touches the handshake path" is a claim about what can influence whether a peer is proven, so `logclient`, `agent` and `nornd` may not import this package at all. Anchoring answers "was this backdated" — a question for an operator reading evidence, not for the code deciding what goes in the peer table in the next 200 milliseconds.
  - **No new dependencies.** `ripemd160` comes from `x/crypto`, already in the graph; `go mod tidy` only reclassified things that were mislabelled indirect. staticcheck objects to the deprecated import, and rightly — so the suppression carries the reason: *the format contains this operation; reading it is not using it*. A reader that refused to compute a `ripemd160` op would simply fail to read proofs other people's tools legitimately write.
  - **The vector suite gained a third provenance, and the D-052 guard caught it immediately.** `anchors/` is produced by neither of Norn's implementations, so `make vectors-check` refused to run until it was declared — which is exactly the check that was added after `make vectors` silently deleted eleven files. It is `KEEP`, not `OWNED`: nothing regenerates it, because regenerating it would defeat the point.
  - Go: 48 packages, 514 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.7b `norn verify --anchored` — and deciding exactly which bytes get stamped.**
  - **`SPEC.md` said "OpenTimestamps proof for the checkpoint at `size`" without saying *which bytes*.** The answer is `sha256` of the checkpoint **body** — origin, size, root — and not the signed note. Two reasons, and the second is the interesting one. A note-level digest would be **unstable**: cosignatures are appended as witnesses answer, so the stamp would stop matching the moment one replied. And it would add nothing — the log is the party an anchor exists to constrain, so binding the claim to the log's own signature proves nothing extra about *when the tree existed*.
  - **The summary is written to be impossible to misread.** Norn walked the operations; it did **not** ask a blockchain whether the commitment is in the block, because it has no blockchain to ask. So the line says `(that the block contains it is not checked here — it needs a bitcoin node)` every time, and a test forbids the phrasings that would sound like more: "confirmed on", "proven at", "verified in". Saying it every time is cheaper than one person concluding otherwise once.
  - **A pending attestation is reported as "not yet anchored".** It is a calendar's *promise* to include a value, not evidence that anybody did, and a reader who treated the two alike would think an unconfirmed stamp meant something.
  - **`--anchored` never fails a verification, and that is D-009 rather than laziness.** A missing, unreadable or mismatched proof is reported and nothing more. A node that refused a perfectly good log because an optional extra was absent would be enforcing the opposite of the decision — and would hand anybody who can delete one file a way to stop a network working.
  - **But a mismatched proof is said loudly**, because "this proof is for a different document" is exactly what a checkpoint edited after stamping looks like. Advisory means it changes nothing the log proves; it does not mean it goes unmentioned.
  - **The vector generator is committed**, so the third-party bytes are reproducible rather than mysterious: `contrib/ots-vector/` holds the script, the six-line `python-bitcoinlib` stub and the reason it exists. There is deliberately **no serializer** in `internal/ots` — Norn reads `.ots` files and never writes them, so the pending-only test prunes a parsed tree rather than growing a writer whose only user would be a test.
  - **The gate line still needs 4.7c and the lab**: *"Checkpoint N has a verifiable OpenTimestamps proof in `anchors/`"* requires somebody to obtain one, which is a calendar round trip (T8's "monitor OTS-stamps checkpoint N"). The checking half is done and the producing half is named rather than assumed.
  - Go: 48 packages, 521 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.7c `norn stamp` — obtaining a proof without growing a serializer.**
  - **The calendar protocol is asymmetric, and that asymmetry is the whole design.** `POST <calendar>/digest` answers with a **bare timestamp tree** — no magic, no version, no file-hash op, because the client knows which digest it asked about. A detached `.ots` is a fixed header followed by exactly those bytes. So building one is **concatenation**, and several calendars merge with the format's own branch marker: `0xff` before every branch but the last.
  - **Which means `internal/ots` still has no serializer.** That was deliberate in 4.7a and it held: a writer built only for this would be checked against its own reader and nothing else. Instead what the concatenation produces is **parsed back and walked** before it is returned — by the reader that was validated against the reference implementation's own bytes. A proof that does not chain is never written, because a proof that does not chain looks exactly like evidence of an edit.
  - **A third-party vector for the calendar's side too.** `calendar-response.bin` is a bare tree written by `python-opentimestamps`, so the fake calendar in the tests answers with bytes this project did not compose.
  - **One calendar down is not a failure**, which is the only reason to list several; **every** calendar down is, because then there is nothing to write. And a calendar that answers with nonsense is refused by name rather than written to disk.
  - **`norn stamp` refuses to stamp a log that does not verify.** A timestamp over a checkpoint this network rejects would be a timestamp on somebody else's document — and it would sit in `anchors/` looking like corroboration.
  - **A test pins the digest rule against the committed vector.** The fixture proof was built by the reference implementation over `sha256(checkpoint body)`; if this build's rule ever drifts, that file stops matching and the test says so. It is the cheapest possible guard on a decision that is otherwise only prose.
  - **Norn does not *upgrade* proofs, and that is a boundary rather than a gap.** Replacing a pending branch with a block attestation means rewriting the tree, which needs the serializer this package is better off without — so `norn stamp` says plainly that the `ots` tool does it. Norn's business is obtaining a proof and reading one.
  - Go: 48 packages, 529 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
  - **The gate line stays open, and precisely why:** `PLAN.md` wants "a verifiable OpenTimestamps proof in `anchors/`", and T8 spells out *"after the Bitcoin proof upgrades"*. Both halves work — obtaining against a calendar, checking against a checkpoint, and failing on a tampered one — but a fresh stamp is a promise, and it becomes evidence a few hours later on the public internet. That is the lab's to run.
- **4.8 the Phase 4 gate audit — two gate lines were false, and one of them was a spec violation in the code that decides the peer table.**
  - **Gate line 1 said "detected via gossip". It was not.** A peer serving a second history went through `askPeers`, failed verification, and was filed as `PEER_REFUSED` — one neighbour being unreachable. So the split-view scenario gossip exists for produced **no fork, no evidence and no freeze**. §11 does not care which source a second history arrived from; a forked peer now raises `FORK`, writes evidence and freezes exactly as a forked log does. The test names the scenario the gate line describes.
  - **Gate line T5 said "proven set unchanged". It was not — and the cause was a normative line ignored.** SPEC §5.3 says in as many words: *"Entries whose `expires` is earlier than **the checkpoint time** are treated as `revoked` at evaluation time… so state stays clock-independent."* The code used **the node's wall clock**, in `proven.FromResult` and, worse, in `Agent.desired` — the function that decides what goes in the peer table. A machine with a fast clock evicted a device early; a slow one kept an expired device; neither said anything. Both now judge expiry against the checkpoint's own time, which is witness time, which is what A11 has said all along.
  - **The fixture had the evidence sitting there.** With the wall clock a year ahead, `contractor` disappeared from the proven set three months before witness time reached its expiry. That is the whole bug in one line of test output, and nothing before this audit had ever asked the question.
  - **With no witness time, expiry is not evaluated at all.** There is no clock, so there is no judgement: the node keeps what it last knew and its freshness reads `unknown`, which is the alarm. Falling back to the wall clock there would have been the one place A11 leaked.
  - **One line closed outright: "Replayed old checkpoint → rejected".** Both mechanisms are tested at three levels — in the verifier, at the node with its own log truncated, and through gossip where a peer cannot roll a node back. T4's `REPLAY` counter is a monitor metric and belongs to Phase 5, which the note says.
  - **One line has nothing behind it, and now says so.** T1b wants a node to freeze on evidence **another node** proved. `GOSSIP.md` lists `GET /norn/evidence`; nothing serves or consumes it. That is a missing feature rather than an untested one, so it is 4.9 rather than a footnote.
  - **The rest are measurements or real hardware**, and each now records exactly which half is true: two log instances and 2·T_g timing, witnesses actually taken down, a real log server killed, a machine with a two-hour offset, a latency in milliseconds, and a calendar round trip that takes hours.
  - Go: 48 packages, 533 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
  - **The pattern held for the third phase running:** a gate made of measurements cannot be closed by a test suite, but auditing it against the code finds the lines whose *substance* was never checked — and two of them were wrong.
- **4.9 evidence propagation — the one place gossip is needed *because* everything looks fine.**
  - **T1b is not a variation on checkpoint gossip, it is the opposite case.** Checkpoints are fetched from peers when the log cannot be used. But node3 in T1b has a **healthy** log: it is being served one consistent history, has no reason to doubt it, and under the checkpoint rule would never ask anybody anything — while two other nodes have already proved that history is a fork. Evidence is needed precisely when nothing looks wrong, so it runs on its own schedule (`T_g`, 30 s) whatever the log is doing. Writing the test first is what made that obvious: it failed exactly as predicted, because a healthy node never asked.
  - **A peer's evidence is believed only because it is checked** — by the same `forkevidence.Check` a stranger would run against the same trust file, which is why 4.6a was built for a sceptical stranger in the first place. Rubbish from a neighbour cannot freeze a network, and a test proves it: otherwise anybody on the tunnel could take the network down with a file of junk. Genuine evidence means there genuinely is a fork, whoever hands it over.
  - **`/norn/evidence` is an exception to 4.5b's rule, and it is an exception by being named.** A node's evidence lives beside its `proven.json` and its proof bundles, none of which is a peer's business — so a *single file* is served from that directory, never the directory. Two traversal attempts are tested.
  - **Newest wins.** A node freezes on the first fork it proves, so a second file means an operator reset with a new trust file and it happened again; the later one is about the network now running.
  - **A node that froze keeps a copy**, so it can pass it on. That is what makes propagation transitive rather than a single hop, and it is what T1b's `≤ 3·T_g` is counting.
  - **A node that learns of a fork before it ever enforced anything enforces nothing at all**, which the test asserts directly: the interface stays empty rather than being filled from a history already proved false.
  - **`T_g` is not the poll interval, and now says so.** Polling the local peer table four times a second is cheap; asking three neighbours over the tunnel that often is neither cheap nor useful.
  - Go: 48 packages, 538 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.1 the monitor's rules — every alert is a sentence, because a page at three in the morning is read by a person.**
  - **`LAB.md` §3's table is the specification, and the tests are it read back.** Governance entries (`signer_*`, `root_*`, `log_key`, `policy`, `params`) are critical because they change *who may say what*; everything else adds or removes a device, which matters less than changing who decides. Every other entry is still reported at info — "a homelab has few", and the one nobody was told about is the one that matters.
  - **Not codes.** `UNPROVEN_PEER on node1: … Something is putting keys on an interface that the log does not prove.` A monitor that pages with `LEVEL=2 CODE=7` has failed at the only job it has, so every rule carries a sentence and a test looks for the node's name in it.
  - **A missing heartbeat says what stops happening**, not that it stopped: *"in watchdog mode that makes its eviction window unbounded"*. Without that an operator files it under "probably fine", which is exactly what it is not.
  - **Anchor lag is info and only ever info**, and the test says why: D-009 makes anchoring advisory, and the monitor is precisely where that would quietly stop being true. A page about a missing anchor trains an operator to treat it as load-bearing.
  - **The escalation is a real distinction.** A checkpoint past `staleness` is a warning; past **3·S** it is critical, because a freeze that has run three times too long is a different event from one that just started — and the alert says which it is rather than repeating the same line louder.
  - **Every alert names its rule**, because the gate line asks for "one notification **per rule**" and nothing can count that otherwise. A test requires the names to be distinct, so a rule that cannot be told from another cannot hide inside it.
  - **Thresholds are fields, not constants.** A homelab changes the heartbeat window or the anchor-lag tolerance without editing Go, and a test sets both to prove it.
  - Go: 49 packages, 548 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.2 watching a network — the monitor is an enrolled peer, not a privileged one.**
  - **It verifies everything it reads with the same `logclient.Verify` a node runs, under the same anchor.** That is not code reuse for its own sake: a monitor that believed something a node would refuse would be reporting on a different network than the one that exists, and its alerts would be about nothing. A node's gossip endpoints are read through `gossip.Peer`, which is the same Fetcher a node uses on a neighbour — so the monitor has no privileged path to anything.
  - **Nothing in a round returns an error.** A monitor that fell over when the thing it monitors fell over would be worse than no monitor, because somebody is relying on it. Every failure becomes an alert and the round carries on; a test runs one with the log gone and every node unreachable and requires alerts to come out.
  - **An unchanged log is silent.** Ninety entries repeated every thirty seconds gets a monitor turned off within the hour, and then it is monitoring nothing — so the log size and the rejection count already described are remembered, and a second round over the same log says nothing new.
  - **The fixture's rejected entries turned out to be a feature.** It contains entries replay refuses, and a log publishing those is its operator misbehaving or running something buggy — the one thing *only* a monitor would ever notice, since every node simply ignores them. The test requires them to be reported.
  - **Evidence is read before the checkpoint**, because a node that has frozen may not be answering anything else usefully, and what it is holding is the loudest thing it could possibly say.
  - **A monitor starting up does not accuse everybody of being down.** A node never yet seen is recorded as seen now, so the heartbeat rule measures silence rather than ignorance.
  - **An architecture test for §3's own words.** "It never sits on the enforcement path": `agent`, `reconcile` and `nornd` may not import the monitor at all. The monitor is allowed to do things a node must not — poll witnesses, hold a receipt inbox, talk to a push service on the internet — and that is safe exactly because nothing it concludes can reach the peer table.
  - Go: 49 packages, 557 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.3 the receipt inbox — and a `uint64` cast that made every receipt look expired.**
  - **SPEC §7 decides the design in one clause**: *"A receipt whose leaf is absent from every checkpoint **cosigned after `deadline`** is non-repudiable proof of log misbehaviour."* **Cosigned after.** The comparison is against witness time, never the monitor's clock — which is exactly what makes the finding non-repudiable rather than an accusation: it does not depend on the accuser's clock being right. A monitor running fast cannot slander an honest log, and a witness outage produces **silence** rather than a false alarm, because with no cosignature nothing can be said to be late. Both are tests.
  - **A latent bug, found by needing the behaviour §7 implies.** `receipt.Check` compared `deadline <= uint64(now.Unix())`. `Unix()` is negative before 1970 and the zero `time.Time` is year 1, so that cast **wrapped** to something enormous and every receipt looked expired — silently, and in the direction that turns a valid promise into a refusal. It compares as signed now, and a zero clock explicitly means *do not judge the deadline*, which is what a caller with no clock it trusts for the purpose actually needs.
  - **Receipts are checked at the door**, because anybody can forward one — so anybody could forward a forgery. A receipt that is not this log's word, or is for another network, is refused and not kept: otherwise the monitor could be made to accuse an honest operator by anyone who can reach it.
  - **A proven misbehaviour stays pending.** The proof does not stop being true because it has been mentioned once, and an operator who was asleep still has to find it in the morning. Honoured receipts are forgotten, which is the only thing that should ever clear one.
  - **The alert says what was promised and what the witnesses vouched for**, with both instants — so the reader can check the claim rather than take it.
  - Go: 49 packages, 565 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.4 outputs — "all optional; stdout always", and the last word is the one worth defending.**
  - **A monitor whose only record of an alert was a push that failed to send has no record at all**, and the operator finds out by not finding out. So stdout is not a fallback, it is unconditional: one JSON object per line, because a log shipper, `jq` and a person at three in the morning can all read that without a parser.
  - **Each sink fails alone, and the test is the point of the file.** A fanout with a broken sink between two working ones must leave both working ones untouched — the phone being unreachable is *exactly* when the file on disk matters. Failures are returned as a list rather than an error, because there is nothing the caller should do differently: the alert has already happened.
  - **A hanging sink is a failing sink.** Every network output is bounded, and the test blocks a server forever to prove the monitor does not block with it.
  - **A fork must not arrive on a phone looking like an enrolment.** `ntfy` priorities are mapped from the level — `urgent`, `high`, `default` — because the whole point of a push is that the important one wakes somebody, and a test requires an info and a critical not to look alike.
  - **The rule name goes in the Home Assistant payload and the Alertmanager labels.** "Turn the office light red on a fork" has to match on *which rule fired*, not parse a sentence; `alertname` and `severity` are the names Alertmanager's own routing trees expect.
  - **Email is split where the honesty is.** The message it builds is tested — headers, the blank line, a subject triageable from a lock screen — and the five-line `net/smtp` call is a seam, because there is no logic behind it worth mocking and rather a lot of configuration only a real server can validate. Header values have their newlines stripped: an alert is not attacker input, but it is *generated* text, and a stray newline in a header is how one message becomes two.
  - **A deadlock in my own test, worth naming because it is the usual one.** `httptest.Server.Close` waits for outstanding handlers, so a handler blocked on a channel closed by a *later* defer hangs the test rather than the code. Defer order is release-then-close.
  - Go: 49 packages, 575 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.5 the status page — and the fixture had a device built to break it.**
  - **The security property is not obvious until you look at what is on the page.** A device's labels are chosen by **whoever enrolled it**, and the suite ships a device called `escaped` whose labels contain `<`, `>` and `&` — a fixture written in Phase 1 for the canonical-JSON vectors, sitting there ready. A page that let a label become markup would be a network where *enrolling a device is enough to rewrite what everybody reads*. Alerts are the same problem from another direction: a subject is a peer's name, a message carries a log operator's origin.
  - **`html/template` is the feature, not a formality**, and the test proves the difference rather than the spelling: swapping it for `text/template` turns both escaping tests red, naming the exact strings that escaped. The awkward label must also still be **readable** — escaped rather than dropped — because an operator who cannot see what a device is actually called has lost the thing the page is for.
  - **Read-only, like every surface Norn puts on the tunnel.** Anything but `GET`/`HEAD` is `405`: a status page answers questions and takes no instructions.
  - **A monitor with nothing to show says so.** "nothing verified yet — this monitor has not managed to read the log since it started" beats a blank screen, which is indistinguishable from a broken one.
  - **The device table is judged at the checkpoint's time**, the same rule §5.3 gave the enforcement path in 4.8 — so the page and the peer table cannot disagree about who is expired.
  - **The recent entries use `plainlog.Describe`**, the renderer `norn status` already uses, so the page and the terminal never describe the same log differently.

- **5.6 `norn monitor` — and one round over the fixture fires four of §3's rules.**
  - **A real run, not a claim:** `monitor -once` against the shipped log produces `new-entry` × 59, `governance-entry` × 16, `rejected-entry` × 54 and `checkpoint-age`, each as a sentence — *"enroll: signer 83eba1f8 enrolled pi-cam (wireguard 651e98a3)"*. The gate line wants one notification per rule during a lab run; this is as close as a repository can get, and it is what makes that countable at all.
  - **A misspelled setting is refused rather than ignored.** `ntfyy = "…"` stops the monitor from starting, because a typo in an output URL is otherwise a monitor that **silently never pages** — and the operator finds out during the incident. The same `Undecoded` check `nornd` uses.
  - **The zero config is a working monitor.** Every output is optional and stdout is unconditional, so the smallest `monitor.toml` that does anything is a trust file and a log.
  - **A dead output does not stop a round, and is not swallowed either.** The failing sink is named on stderr while stdout still gets everything — an output nobody knows is broken is an output nobody is reading.
  - **Failing to bind the status page *is* fatal**, and the asymmetry is deliberate: an operator who asked for a page and did not get one goes looking for it during an incident, which is the worst possible moment to discover a typo in a port. A failed *delivery* is news; a page that was never going to exist is a misconfiguration.
  - **A name collision the compiler caught**, worth a line only because it is the shape of thing a test file can hide: the test's helper and the config struct were both `monitorConfig`.
  - Go: 49 packages, 586 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.7 the Phase 5 gate audit — one line closed, one command missing, and the rest honestly a lab's.**
  - **The phase divides cleanly and the audit says which side each line falls on.** Five gate lines: one is pure lab (`make demos` — `lab/` and `demo/` are empty directories, and their contents are containers, a patched Headscale and NetBird, and rendered tapes), three have complete software halves, and one names a command that does not exist.
  - **`norn audit --by` is missing, and two documents name it.** `ENROLLMENT.md` R2 spells it out — *"`norn audit --by <kid> --since <t>` lists everything that key signed"* — and `LAB.md` D2 uses it in the demo. It is the first thing an operator runs when a signer key is stolen: not "revoke it", but **"what did they do with it"**. That is a missing feature rather than an untested one, so it is 5.8 rather than a footnote — the same call the 4.8 audit made about `/norn/evidence`.
  - **D3 closes outright**, which is the first Phase 5 line to close: a node with an unreachable log adopts a peer's checkpoint (4.5d), a fork produces evidence on the node that caught it *and* on every node it tells (4.6, 4.9), and `norn verify-evidence` checks that evidence offline against a trust file alone, refusing four kinds of fabrication (4.6a). Running it with tapes is the lab's, but no line of it is unproven.
  - **D1's software half is complete and was built in Phase 3**, before the demo existed to ask for it: owner mode never installs an unproven key, an injected key is gone after exactly one cycle, `CONTROL_PLANE_FIGHT` fires on a key that keeps coming back, and quarantine takes the interface out of service. What the demo adds is the *measurement* and the three control planes.
  - **D2's point survives the missing command.** "A valid-looking enrolment is still loud" is already true: the monitor reports every entry at info and every governance entry as critical, so the rogue enrolment appears whether or not anybody runs `audit`. The command is for the *next* question.
  - **Nothing was ticked on the strength of a plausible-sounding half.** Four lines stay open with a sentence each saying exactly which half is true and which needs a container, a phone or a stopwatch.
  - A documentation checkpoint: no code changed, and the suite stands at 49 packages, 586 tests, green.

- **5.8 `norn audit --by` — the question a revoked key cannot answer.**
  - **R2 puts this *before* the `signer_remove`, and that ordering is the point.** Removing a stolen key stops the next thing; this finds the last things. It is also the one answer a transparency log exists to make possible: a control plane with an admin console can tell you the current state, but it cannot tell you what a key did three weeks ago, because nothing kept the receipts. Over the fixture it reports *"entry 11 · signer 83eba1f8 revoked pi-cam (decommissioned)"* and seventeen others.
  - **A malformed key id is refused, and that is the most important line in the file.** `"This key signed nothing"` is the most dangerous wrong answer this command can give, and a typo would produce it **every time** — quietly, reassuringly, and at exactly the moment somebody is deciding whether they have a problem. Sixteen hex characters or an error.
  - **`--since` carries a warning about itself.** It filters on `ts`, which is set by *whoever signed the entry* — so if the key was stolen, the thief controls that field and can place an entry outside the window. The command says so on screen and tells the operator to run without it. A convenience that can hide evidence has to admit it.
  - **Rejected entries are shown under `-all`, marked as such.** A stolen key's *attempts* matter whether or not they worked, and "it is in the log, and it changed nothing" is a different fact from "it never happened" — the log carries both, which is the whole reason it is a log.
  - **A key that signed nothing gets a plain sentence**, not an empty list. After a scare, that is the answer somebody is hoping for, and it should read like an answer rather than like a command that failed to find its input.
  - Go: 49 packages, 592 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **6.1 freezing `SPEC.md` — two promises the document was still making, and one it could finally keep.**
  - **§13 said "Resolution is pending a decision" eight checkpoints after the decision.** The `enroll` device-signature problem was settled in 4.1c — the device signs its own **request**, the signer embeds it verbatim — and §4.3, §5.1 and §16 were all rewritten that way. Only §13 never heard. A reader implementing from the frozen spec would have found a live open item pointing at a `BUILD-LOG` entry that closes it.
  - **§12.1 still owed a fork-evidence vector "by Phase 4".** Phase 4 shipped without it, so the last thing `GOSSIP.md` sends to witness operators and blog posts was the one artifact only the implementation that reads it had ever produced. `fork/evidence.cbor` and `fork/conflicting.checkpoint` now ship: a second branch of the same network diverging at entry 40, signed by the same log key and cosigned by the same witnesses, checkable with nothing but `trust.cbor`.
  - **The generator's first attempt was wrong, and 4.6a's guard caught it.** I built the "fork" as `entries[:40]` — a **prefix**, not a fork — and `forkevidence.Build` refused it by name: *"these two heads agree at size 40; a log and its own earlier self is not a fork"*. That guard was written precisely so a node could not make this accusation; it turns out it also stops the project shipping a vector that proves nothing.
  - **"Not vectored" is now an empty list**, and what replaced it is two caveats rather than a silence: the fixture's cosignatures prove the *format* (an unmodified witness is proven separately, in `internal/interop`), and the fork evidence proves the *artifact is checkable* — it is not evidence that any log has ever equivocated. A vector proves a format, not a fact about the world.
  - **A mechanical guard, so this cannot happen again.** A spec marked `Status: **v1**` may not contain `NOT YET IMPLEMENTED`, `pending a decision`, `TODO`, `TBD` or an `owed by | Phase` row. Both of the things this checkpoint found were of exactly that shape, and both had been true when written — which is what makes them invisible. The test names the line number.
  - **Frozen is defined where a reader will look**: "a second implementation may be written against this document and the vectors beside it, and neither will change under it."
  - Go: 49 packages, 595 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green — the Go half now covering `fork/` as well.

- **6.2 the vectors stand alone — and the document a second implementer reads first was wrong.**
  - **The suite's README said the whole directory came from the Python reference.** By then `served/`, `proofs/` and `fork/` came from this project's Go and `anchors/` from `python-opentimestamps`. Provenance is the *first* thing a reader needs, because it decides how much a vector proves — a file the Go must match is a different kind of evidence from one the Go produced — and the one sentence stating it had quietly become false. Three provenances, in a table, with what each is worth.
  - **Sixteen of seventeen top-level entries were undescribed.** The README was thirteen lines, twelve of which were about `anchors/`, because that was the last thing added and the only thing anybody had thought to explain. A test now fails if anything in the directory is unnamed in the README: *a file a second implementer cannot identify is one they cannot use*.
  - **`SHA256SUMS`, for the reader who does not have the generators.** `make vectors-check` regenerates and diffs, which catches a vector that drifted from its generator; checksums catch one edited by hand — and fail with the *filename*, which a diff of 293 files does not.
  - **The generator hashed its own temporary file.** `xargs … > SHA256SUMS.tmp` creates the file before `find` lists the directory, so the first run produced 294 entries for 293 files, and the guard caught it immediately. The temp file now lives outside the tree being hashed.
  - **The guard's first catch was the sentence documenting it.** I wrote the `SHA256SUMS` section into the README *after* generating the sums, so the README's own hash changed and the test failed by name. Which is the behaviour asked for, arriving about ninety seconds after it was written.
  - **And `vectors-check` refused the new file until it was declared**, exactly as it refused `anchors/` in 4.7a. `SHA256SUMS` is `KEEP`: nothing generates it from the vectors, it is generated *from* them.
  - **"Using them" is an ordering, not an inventory.** Start where nothing is needed but Ed25519 and SHA-256, then replay, then tiles — and the half most implementations get wrong is named as such: `trust-bad/`, `requests-bad/` and `log/*.reason` are files that **must be refused**, for the stated reason.

- **6.3 the README — the one document nothing was checking.**
  - **It said "planning complete, not yet code" through six phases of work.** Written 2026-09-18 and true for about a day. By the time anyone read it there were 49 packages and 610 tests, a frozen spec and a vector suite with three provenances, and the first line a stranger saw said none of that existed. A spec is checked by its vectors and a plan by its gates; the README had nothing, so it drifted in the one direction nobody notices — *downwards*, understating what was there, which is the polite failure but still a false statement.
  - **So the countable claims are now checked on every build** (`internal/readmedoc`). The package counts, the test count, and the **14 of 38 gate lines** ratio are parsed out of the prose and compared against the tree and against `PLAN.md`. A README that states no countable claim fails too: silence is how the last one drifted.
  - **It caught its own drift inside a minute.** Writing the checks added eleven test functions, so the number the README had just been given — 609 — was wrong before the file was saved. 610. That is the whole argument for the package, delivered unprompted.
  - **The first checker was brittle about line wrapping** and demanded `14 of 38 gate lines` on one line with no emphasis markers. That is a checker dictating prose. The regexes now tolerate wraps and `*`/`_`, because the author of a README should be free to wrap a sentence.
  - **The commands in it are run, and their output is checked.** `Transcripts` parses the `console` blocks; a test builds `cmd/norn` into a temp dir, runs every `./norn …` line from the repo root, and fails if one errors or stops printing a line the README promises. A quickstart that no longer works is worse than none — it teaches a stranger to expect the wrong thing, at the exact moment they are deciding whether to keep reading.
  - **The quickstart leads with the fork, not the happy path.** `norn verify` on the served vectors is table stakes; `norn verify-evidence` on `fork/evidence.cbor` prints **FORK PROVEN** offline, from a trust anchor alone, needing neither the network running nor the accuser trusted. That is the thesis in one command, and it runs in a fresh clone with no daemon.
  - **Five mutations, five reds**: a broken vector path, an output line drifted by one entry, "blockchain" in the tagline, a rival renamed out of the comparison, and `production-ready` added while gates are open. Restored, green.
  - **The comparison table is by product, not by camp.** `PLAN.md` argued in camps — coordination servers, offline PKI, Tailnet Lock — which is the right shape for deciding what to build and the wrong one for a reader asking *should I use this instead of what I have*. Eight questions against Tailnet Lock / Nebula / NetBird / Norn, sourced to `PRIOR-ART.md`, and the last row is **"would I run it on something that matters today?"** — where Norn's answer is **no**, and the other three say yes.
  - **The gate line stays open.** It asks for review by somebody who has run at least two of them; I have run none of the three. The table says so in the text underneath it rather than quietly hoping nobody asks.

- **6.5l-i — §6 stops deferring what a reader cannot fetch.**
  - **The largest finding of run 11 was not a rule, it was a hole in the handover.** §6 deferred the `tlog-tiles` layout — "this one neither restates them nor varies them" — and that C2SP document is not in the sealed copy, so a reader holding `SPEC.md` and `vectors/` **could not use `served/`, `served-bad/`, `served-*/` or `detect/` at all**. Run 11 derived `tile/entries/000.p/126` from outside knowledge and said so. §7 restates the note framing and §9.1 the policy grammar, both with the same sentence — "a verifier cannot be written without it". §6 now does too: the three-digit group form, the `x` prefix, the `.p/<W>` partial suffix, and the two paths this fixture actually serves.
  - **The worked examples are checked against the log.** A path in the document that went stale when the fixture grew would teach the wrong shape, so the guard opens `served/` and requires every concrete tile path §6 writes down to be a file that exists. It caught three of my own first-draft examples — counterfactual "at 256 entries those become…" paths — and they came out.
  - **D11: "the checkpoint body" meant two byte ranges one bullet apart.** §6 defines it as exactly three lines, then four bullets later stamps "the checkpoint **body** — the note text up to and including the blank line". Those digests differ (`d99a0118…` vs `4e2492c7…`), §7 uses the *first* range for signed text, and `anchors/checkpoint-126.ots` stamps the second. §6 now names the bytes — three body lines **plus** the blank line, four `0x0A` — and says plainly that three different ranges are in play.
  - **`anchors/` had no instructions at all.** No `cases.json`, no mention in §12.1 in either list, a §12 row that repeated the ambiguous word *without* §6's qualifier, and an OpenTimestamps container format this document describes nowhere. It now ships a manifest stating each proof's digest in hex and naming the bytes that produce it, so the rule is checkable **without parsing OpenTimestamps** — and the test recomputes the digest from the named bytes, so the manifest cannot drift when the tree moves.
  - **Why that one is worth the care**: anchoring is advisory (D-009), so a reader who computes a neighbouring range never matches a stamp and **never says so**. Silent, in the one mechanism whose job is to constrain the log operator about time. One control, red: hashing §7's range instead fails the manifest test and four others.
  - **D13**: §4.0 opened by naming three artifacts and governs five; proof bundles and fork evidence are in the first sentence now, not two paragraphs down.
  - **`make test` grew a `-timeout`.** `cmd/norn` takes about five minutes and hit Go's default per-package 600s once, under the load of the gate run. A suite that flakes under load teaches people to re-run rather than to read.

- **6.5l-ii-a — condition 3 is about the key the node installs, not the key the bundle carries.**
  - **D14 was a quiet peer-dropper.** §8.1 condition 3 reads "replay … yields the device as `active` with that `wg_pk`", and *that* has no antecedent in its own sentence. The nearest candidate is the bundle's, because the whole list is about a bundle — and `rotate` is routine, while §5.3 deliberately leaves `since_index` on the entry that made the device active. So for **any device that has ever rotated**, the bundle proving it is older than its key. The strict reading drops those peers in the ordinary course of operation, and passed all 622 checks doing it.
  - **Nothing joined the two halves.** `proofs/cases.json` exercises the "Checking a bundle" procedure, which does not include conditions 1–4; `proven/cases.json` was computed from `state.json` alone. `proven/cases.json` now carries a `bundles` map: per active device, the index its bundle proves, the key that entry carries, the key replay yields, and whether the two differ. `nas` is the one where they do — bundle at index 1 (`116f00fa…`), state `2a955fd2…` after `log/35-rotate-nas` — and `nas` is proven in all four cases.
  - **The facts come from the replay, not from the `.bundle` files.** Those are written by the Go generator, so a reader checking one against the other is checking two implementations against each other. The Go guard decodes the shipped bundle and requires it to agree with what the Python-written manifest claims.
  - **Two controls.** Modelling the strict reader against the shipped vectors drops `nas`, which the proven set requires — so the *vectors* catch it, which is what run 11 said nothing did. Erasing the separation in the manifest turns the in-repo guard red.
  - **A generator bug caught on the way**: `wg_pk` is bytes in the in-memory state and hex only once serialised, so comparing the two reported *every* device as rotated — a separation that separates nothing. Both sides are normalised now.

- **6.5l-ii-b — a local limit the network could raise was not a local limit.**
  - **D-060 taken: `hard_stale` is tested before `fresh`.** The two predicates overlap whenever `S_hard < elapsed ≤ S`, and the implementation tested `fresh` first — which made a node's own hard limit **unreachable** whenever the network's `staleness` was at or above it. `staleness` moves by root quorum, so the roots could lift every node's local hard stop without touching a single node's configuration. §8.1 cites D-034 as the reason `S_hard` is local rather than a `params` key; a limit the network can raise is not local, and that is the whole argument.
  - **The fixture builds the trap without anyone arranging it.** Genesis sets `staleness: 600`, `log/29-params` raises it to 900, and `nornd` accepts any `S_hard` at or above 600 — so `S_hard = 700`, a sensible choice while `S` was 600, lands in the overlap permanently and silently. This was never hypothetical.
  - **Why the suite could not see it**: every `detect/staleness.json` case had `hard` 3600 *above* `staleness` 900, so the overlap was unreachable and run 11's `HardBeforeFresh` variant passed all 622 checks. Two cases now reach it — `hard-below-staleness` (elapsed 800, hard 700 → `hard_stale`) and `at-the-hard-boundary` (elapsed 700 → `fresh`, because the rule is `> S_hard` like every other boundary here).
  - **One control, and it fails in the right place.** Putting `fresh` back first turns `TestTheStalenessLadderIsVectored` red — the *vector-driven* test, which is the cross-implementation contract, not an in-repo assertion.
  - **Three documents had to move together.** §8.1 now states that the states are tested in the order given and that `hard_stale` comes first, with the reason. `GOSSIP.md`'s state machine drew `degraded` as `S < age ≤ S_hard`, which silently assumes `S_hard > S`; it is reordered, and says what happens when the node's limit is the tighter one. `cmd/nornd`'s floor stays but is re-framed as a pure typo guard — it was refusing values "below the §4.5 staleness default" on the grounds that they "would drop every peer before the network had even called the view degraded", which is precisely the configuration D-060 makes legitimate.
  - **The cost, stated plainly**: a node in the overlap has no `degraded` band — it goes from `fresh` straight to `hard_stale` at its own limit. That is what asking for a tight hard limit means.

- **6.5l-iii — §9.1 argued from two rules it never stated, and one of them nobody enforced.**
  - **Measured before touching anything, because a report is a reader too.** The `log lines` row justified "at most one" from two premises: a `log` line must name `norn.trust.log_key`, and *no key may appear twice in one policy*. Neither premise was a rule anywhere in the table. Probing both parsers: **one key under two names** — Go refuses, the reference **accepts**; **two `log` lines** — *both accept*; **U+00A0 in a URL** — Go accepts, the reference **refuses**. Two live divergences and one rule enforced by nobody, none of it visible to any vector.
  - **Why that is not cosmetic.** Policy text rides verbatim in a `policy` log entry, which is `decode` unless it parses. A policy one implementation refuses and the other applies splits `state.json` — a byte-identical MUST — and with it `rejected`, and with it every later checkpoint's quorum. One node's `group home 2 a b` is met by a single witness answering once; the other never got the policy at all. That is precisely the failure the `group` members row exists to prevent, one level down and unwritten.
  - **D12 was the same shape.** §9.1 named `\r`, `\v`, `\f` after an em dash, which reads as examples; the reference used Python's Unicode-aware `isspace`, so a non-breaking space was an error there and content in Go. The set is **closed** now — those three bytes, every other byte is content — and the reference's field splitter had to change with it, since `str.split()` with no argument also splits on Unicode spaces and would have turned the NBSP into a separator the moment it stopped being refused.
  - **Four vectors, one of them a positive.** `bad-duplicate-key`, `bad-two-log-lines` (two *different* keys, so uniqueness cannot catch it), `bad-form-feed` (the third banned byte), and `witness-url-nbsp`, which **MUST parse** — the complement is what makes "closed" mean something.
  - **Three controls, three reds, each through `TestEveryPolicyGrammarCaseIsDecidedTheSameWay`** — the test that reads `policies/cases.json`, so it is the cross-implementation contract failing rather than an in-repo assertion.
  - **D5 got the honest treatment rather than a vector.** `bad-empty-group` is `group g all` — three fields — so the field-count row refuses it before "at least one member" is reached. Both readings refuse, so there is no hazard; the fault was §12.1 crediting it to the members rule. It now says which rule actually refuses it and that the other half is subsumed.
  - **A guard caught my own edit.** Rewriting the separators row broke the declared phrase for `bad-vertical-tab` and `bad-carriage-return`, and `TestEveryNegativeDeclaresTheSectionThatStatesItsRule` failed until both were repointed — which is exactly what 6.5k-i-b built it to do.

- **6.5l-iv-a — a declared key id is a claim, and it binds nothing unless somebody recomputes it.**
  - **D3 is §12.1 claiming a treatment it had not given.** Two rows apart, the table said `11`, `13` and `16` each carry their damaged key in the **policy** so only the named rule can refuse them — and then credited `37-log-key-id-mismatch` with the same isolation. Measured: `11`, `13`, `16` propagate; `37` carried `1ea4a666` in `log_key` and left `bea4a666` in its policy, so the correspondence rule refused it first and `TrustNoKeyIDCheck` — a loader that takes a declared id as an opaque label, which is the *natural* thing to do since the id is also what you match on — passed all 622 checks.
  - **D9 is what that unlocks.** §7 makes the id bind the name, and §10's cosignature match leans on it: name **and** id, either half implying the other. That implication holds only while the id is recomputed. Drop the recomputation and match lines on the id alone, and a reader has verified nothing about *who* signed — the dual of the attack §10 refuses in the other direction. No shipped file carried a mis-declared **witness** id, so the pair was unreachable.
  - **`37` now propagates, and `39-witness-key-id-mismatch` covers the other half** — one hex digit of `pi`'s declared id changed, in the `witnesses` array *and* in the policy's `witness` line, so the correspondence rule agrees and only the recompute can refuse it. One control: deleting the id check accepts **both**, through `TestEveryDamagedAnchorIsRejected`, which reads the manifest.
  - **And the declaration guard found the rule was never written down.** §9.1 said "the algorithm is part of the key id" — a statement *about* the id, not an instruction to check it — so `TestEveryNegativeDeclaresTheSectionThatStatesItsRule` refused both cases until §9.1 said a reader MUST **recompute** `SHA-256(name ‖ 0x0A ‖ alg ‖ pk)[0:4]` and refuse a file whose declared id differs. The same shape as D2 one section over: a rule the vectors depend on that the section never contained.

- **6.5l-iv-b — the origin comparison is equality, and nothing had said so in a way a file could check.**
  - **Three readings, all measured green before the fix.** `OriginPrefixMatch`, `OriginCaseFold` and `LogKeyNamePrefix` each passed the suite, because **every** origin negative used an *unrelated* origin: `03-wrong-origin` and `10-wrong-body-origin` name another network, `trust-bad/11` uses `someone/else`, `13` uses `norn/aaaa…`. None of them is a prefix or a case variant of this network's origin, so none of them separates equality from a loose match.
  - **Why it is worth a vector even at low severity.** §10 spends a paragraph arguing the cosignature match must be name **and** key id, because a name-only match hands anyone a **veto** — and ships `served-name-collision/` to pin it. The origin comparison is the same argument from the other side: a loose match hands an attacker an **acceptance**. One of the two was pinned and the other was not.
  - **Three cases, each the honest artifact with one string changed.** `21-origin-with-a-suffix` (`…mbqgea` — this origin plus one base32 character), `22-origin-case-changed` (`norn/Nzxx…` — one letter in the other case), both signed under the correct note name and cosigned exactly as correctly as the real head, so only §10's comparison can refuse them. `trust-bad/40-log-key-name-suffix` does the same for §9.1's "the note name MUST equal `origin`" — and carries the damaged key in the policy too, which is the lesson `37` taught one checkpoint ago.
  - **Three controls, three reds, one case each**, all through the manifest-driven harnesses.
  - **A prose count had drifted twice, so it now has a guard.** The vectors README said "twenty checkpoints" when twenty-two shipped. `TestTheVectorsREADMEStatesTheRightCounts` reads the directory and accepts the count in digits or in words — a number in prose that nothing checks is a claim, and §12.1 opens by saying an inaccurate coverage claim is the same failure wearing a different hat.

- **6.5l-iv-c — a root quorum that authorises itself.**
  - **D8: §4.2's "the state *before* this entry" was exercised by nothing.** Not one entry in 126 was signed by a key it was itself adding, so `ResolveKeysAfterEntry` — let a `root_add`'s incoming key count toward its own quorum — passed all 622 checks. The rule is stated once, in a sentence about signature resolution, and §9.4 makes the identical ordering argument for log keys in a whole section ("a stolen log key therefore cannot name its own successor"). The root-key version of that argument had no vector.
  - **`126-bad-root_add-counts-its-own-key`.** `root_thr` is 3 and the roots are root1–root3; the entry carries **two** valid root signatures and a third by `root4`, the key in its own body. Under §4.2 that is two of three → `threshold`. Under the other reading it is three of four and the entry applies — a quorum that bootstraps itself, which is one stolen root key short of a takeover.
  - **My first measurement was wrong and I caught it.** The first mutation added the incoming key to `st.Roots` and deferred a delete, which ran *after* the handler had legitimately added it — so the state diverged and the suite went red for a reason that had nothing to do with the rule. A false red is as bad as a false green. Redone against a non-destructive copy of the roots map, the variant passed, which is the measurement D8 actually claims.
  - **One control, one entry**: `entry 126 … got reason "", want "threshold"`, through the manifest.
  - **The tile-path guard earned itself back.** Growing the log to 127 broke §6's worked example `tile/0/000.p/126`, and `TestTheTilePathsAreRestatedAndTheirExamplesResolve` — written one checkpoint ago precisely because a stale path teaches the wrong shape — failed until both examples were updated. It cost nothing to write and caught a real drift the first time the log moved.

- **6.5l-v — four caps counted in bytes, and four vectors that could not tell bytes from characters.**
  - **D6, measured directly rather than by mutation.** Every over-long negative was pure ASCII and exactly one byte past its cap — `116-bad-revoke-reason-too-long` at 257/257, `83-bad-text-too-long` at 4097/4097, `genesis/14-name-too-long` at 65/65. Over the cap **both** ways, so a reader counting runes refuses them too and nothing separates the readings. That is not a mutation result; it is arithmetic on the shipped bytes.
  - **§12.1 had already recorded finding and fixing exactly this** for §4.0's map-key cap in run 9 — "both were ASCII until run 9, so 23 bytes and 23 characters gave the same answer everywhere" — and the fix was not carried to the four field caps. The lesson was written down and not generalised, which is the same failure 6.5k-i-b's declaration guard was built to stop.
  - **All four are multi-byte now**: 129 × `é` for `reason` (258 bytes, 129 characters) and for label values, 2049 for `text`, 33 for both the genesis and trust-file `name`. Over the cap in bytes and well under it in runes, so the two readings now disagree — and the test asserts that property rather than the byte count, so a future edit that quietly returns them to ASCII fails.
  - **Why `reason` is the one that matters.** It is free-form human text on a `revoke`, and 256 bytes is about 85 CJK characters. A revocation written in any non-Latin script and run past the cap is `decode` for one reader and accepted by the other — so the two disagree about whether the device is revoked at all, and §5.3 makes `state.json` a byte-identical MUST.
  - **D4, and a new directory.** §6 is emphatic that a checkpoint's size line has exactly one spelling; §7 showed the receipt's deadline as a bare `<uint>` with no rule and no vector, and `ReceiptDeadlineLax` passed. `receipt-bad/leading-zero.note` is the same receipt with one leading zero, **re-signed**, so its signature is impeccable and only the spelling is wrong. A receipt is offered as non-repudiable proof of log misbehaviour, which means two parties will one day compare two copies of one.
  - **A test I wrote was wrong and the run said so.** My first "only one thing differs" assertion compared the whole signed notes, which necessarily differ because the negative is re-signed over changed text. It now compares the note **texts** and separately verifies the vector's own signature — which is the stronger claim anyway.
  - **Two controls, two reds**: counting runes accepts `116` through the manifest; dropping the canonicality check accepts the padded receipt.

- **6.5l-vi-a — the suite was scoring against a vocabulary the document does not have.**
  - **D10 is the one defect that is about the contract rather than a rule.** §5.2 is a closed, normative list of sixteen reason codes — for **replay**, and nothing else. The trust file, the policy parser, the note and checkpoint parsers, the bundle and evidence checkers, the request decoder and the CBOR profile all refuse without one. And every negative in the suite carries a `says` string: `simple value`, `deeper than`, `can only speak for what it holds`. **None of those phrases is in `SPEC.md`.**
  - **Run 11 said what that cost it**: it reverse-engineered its error vocabulary out of `cases.json` to make the suite pass. For those checks the suite measured its ability to read a JSON file, not the specification — which is the exact inversion this whole exercise exists to prevent.
  - **§12 now states what the field is**: a **semantic hint**, the rule in words, *not the bytes your implementation must produce* — map it by hand, once. And it says what `says` is genuinely for, which is the discipline it enforces *here*: a negative must be refused for its own rule and nothing else, and a phrase naming that rule is how the generators and the placement guards check it.
  - **The honest half.** Saying "it's only a hint" would quietly demote three real rules, so §12.1 names them: §10's **signature-decides-before-origin**, and §11.1's two step orderings. Outside replay both readings refuse, a black-box run sees one event either way, and the ordering is visible *only in the words*. They are pinned by a JSON file and not by this document, and now the document admits it.
  - **What is left is a decision, not work**: §5.2 is the artefact that would fix it, and extending a closed vocabulary to the verifier and evidence layers adds normative surface to a frozen v1. That is 6.5l-vi-b, below.
  - **"Who this is not for" is five paragraphs of reasons to close the tab**, including the one that costs most: Norn's failure direction is to refuse peers it cannot prove, so a log outage plus a strict policy is an outage. A reader who needs the network up more than correct should learn that from me, in the README, and not from their own network at 03:00.


- **6.5l-ii — the freshness table is read top to bottom, and a bundle proves the enrolment, not the current key.**
  - *Superseded on D1 at reconciliation (2026-09-30): this session read the table `fresh` first; D-060 — decided by the user the same week, but never pushed, so this session could not see it — tests `hard_stale` first, and that is what ships. Its D14 work and its reasoning are kept. See **Reconciliation** below.*
  - **D1: `fresh` and `hard_stale` were two separate conditions with nothing saying which wins.** Both hold whenever `S_hard < elapsed ≤ S`, and every `staleness.json` case had `hard` 3600 over `staleness` 900, where that range is empty. That is why `HardBeforeFresh` passed all 622 checks. The range is not exotic. `S` is a `params` key a root quorum can raise, `S_hard` is the node's own, and this suite's log raises `staleness` from 600 to 900 at `29-params`. A node that picked 700 while `S` was 600 lives in the overlap from then on: one reading keeps its peers, the other drops them all.
  - **The order was already decided, just never written down.** Both implementations test `fresh` first. GOSSIP.md's reason for refusing an `S_hard` below the default was that it "would drop every peer before the network had even called the view degraded", and that reason is the rule. §8.1 now says the state is the **first row, read top to bottom, whose condition holds**, and says the consequence plainly: a node never drops its peers while the network calls its view fresh. It also says why letting `S` win costs nothing: the root signatures that raise `staleness` could just as well enroll a device. I did not stop to ask about this one. Nothing about the protocol changed, and no implementation's behaviour changed; the document now says what the code and the design notes already agreed on.
  - **Two vectors, with the fixture's own numbers.** `hard-below-staleness-still-fresh` (`hard` 700, `elapsed` 800) is `fresh`. `hard-below-staleness-past-staleness` (`elapsed` 901) is `hard_stale`, skipping `degraded`, which catches the other lax reading: ignoring an `S_hard` below `S`. **Two controls, two reds, one case each**: testing `hard_stale` first fails only the first case, and ignoring an `S_hard` below `S` fails only the second. A new test requires the file to *keep* a case in the overlap, because a later edit to `staleness` could move it out and leave every case green with the order unpinned again.
  - **D14: condition 3 said "active with that `wg_pk`", and "that" had nothing to refer to.** A bundle proves the entry that made its device active. `rotate` leaves `since_index` on the enrol, so for any device that has rotated the bundle's entry names the key it was *enrolled* with. `proofs/nas.bundle` proves index 1 with `116f00fa…`, and replay gives `2a955fd2…`. Under the strict reading `nas` is dropped, and so is every peer that has ever rotated. That is routine operation, and the node would not say anything about it.
  - **No code had the bug.** I checked for one before writing anything. Nothing in `cmd/` or `internal/` reads a key out of a bundle; `nornd` installs from replay. The gap was that no file joined a bundle to the key a node installs, so a second implementer who read it strictly passed every check. `proofs/cases.json` now carries `installs`, the replayed key for each shipped bundle. The test checks every bundle under `trust.cbor`, requires `installs` to match `state.json`, and fails if no bundle's entry key differs from the installed one. That last check is the one that matters: the vector only means something while the two readings disagree on it. §8.1 now says the node installs **the `wg_pk` replay gives**, adds a paragraph on what a bundle proves about a key and what it does not, and quotes both of `nas`'s keys. A second test fails if those quotations go stale.
  - **Controls.** Writing the strict reading's key into `installs[nas]` fails both new proof tests. Deleting either new phrase from §8.1 fails its row in `TestEveryVectoredRuleIsStatedWhereItLives`. Then everything was restored and is green. The README's self-checked count went 744 → 747 the moment the tests existed, and the docclaims guard caught it.
  - **One thing I left alone.** `nornd` still refuses `S_hard` below 600 (the §4.5 default) and does not warn when the network later raises `S` above the value it holds. With the order written down that is safe. A log line saying "this node will now go from `fresh` straight to `hard_stale`" would still be kind to an operator, and belongs with the Phase 3 lab work rather than in a spec checkpoint.

- **6.5l-iii — the policy grammar's last three, and two of them were live disagreements between this repository's parsers.**
  - **D12 was rated low, and it was the worst of the three.** §9.1 said "any other whitespace character … `\r`, `\v`, `\f`", and the em dash reads as examples. Checking what each implementation does, not what the report guessed: the Go parser refused exactly those three characters, and the Python reference refused all 26 that `str.isspace()` knows and that can occur in a line. U+00A0, U+2028, U+3000 and 20 more were content on one side and an error on the other. A `policy` entry's text is replayed (§5.1), so one no-break space made a `decode` on one node and a new quorum on the other, and no vector could tell them apart. §9.1 now **closes the set** at `0x0B`, `0x0C` and `0x0D`, and says everything else is content. That is Go's reading and the one run 11 proposed. Python now splits on space and tab only; `str.split()` with no argument had also been *splitting* on U+00A0.
  - **D2: the rule §9.1 argued from was written nowhere, and the two parsers disagreed on it too.** The `log lines` row said a second `log` line cannot be written because "no key may appear twice in one policy". Go enforced that across `log` and `witness` lines, Python not at all, so `witness a K` / `witness b K` was `decode` on one side and a policy on the other. It matters because one cosignature line (name and key id) then matches both names, and a single witness answering once meets `group home 2 a b`. That is the same hazard the `group` members row already closes for `pi pi`. There is now a `keys` row, and it also says what the rule cannot do: two *different* keys held by one operator are two witnesses as far as any policy can tell.
  - **Two negatives, because the rule spans directives.** `bad-duplicate-key` (one key, two witness names) and `bad-log-key-as-witness`. A parser that checks keys only between `witness` lines refuses the first and accepts the second. That was a control, and exactly one vector went red. The positive side needed saying too: `two-log-lines` MUST parse. The grammar allows it, a trust file cannot carry it (each line must be `log_key`, and the key rule refuses a repeat), and a `policy` entry has no such correspondence. A policy naming a log's old and new key is what a rotation looks like from outside, and a parser enforcing "at most one" refuses it.
  - **D5 is an admission, stated in the row itself.** "At least one member" is the field count's rule: `group g all` has three fields and is refused before the members row is reached. Relaxing the field count by one is the control, and it fails `bad-empty-group` alone. The members row now says so, and `bad-empty-group` declares the field count phrase instead of the table's opening sentence.
  - **The control that found a hole in my own fix.** Demoting `\f` to ordinary content left every vector green. `quorum\fpi` is then one field, an unknown keyword, refused anyway. Only a unit test in `internal/policy` noticed, and a second implementer never receives that test. The same was true of `bad-vertical-tab` and `bad-carriage-return` all along: run 10's D9 fix moved the stray character to a place where the **separator** reading parses, and so let the **content** reading through. Each character now ships twice. The `-in-url` copies put it inside a URL, where content parses and a split makes five fields. A new test proves each placement against its lax reading, and demoting all three characters to content now fails three manifest cases, one each.
  - **The declarations got sharper while I was there.** Eleven `policies/` cases declared the table's opening sentence, which every case in the directory satisfies, so the declaration said nothing. Each now names the row that states its rule. Every negative in `policies/` points at its own phrase, and deleting the key rule's phrase from §9.1 fails the placement test.
  - **Twelve controls.** Seven on the Go parser: no key rule, the key rule between `witness` lines only, Unicode whitespace as an error, Unicode whitespace as a separator, at most one `log` line, `\f` as content, and a field count relaxed by one. Two re-runs of the content reading once the `-in-url` vectors existed. Two on the Python reference's old behaviours, where the generator's oracle refuses `nbsp-is-content`. One on the key rule's declaration. All twelve end red in the manifest or the placement test. The one that first went red only in a unit test is the reason six vectors exist instead of three. `policies/` is 31 cases, up from 23.

- **Reconciliation — two sessions did 6.5l-ii and 6.5l-iii in parallel, and disagreed twice (2026-09-30).**
  - **How it happened.** This machine's session worked 6.5l-ii through 6.5l-vi-a and never pushed. A second session, working from `origin/master`, did 6.5l-ii and 6.5l-iii independently and was merged as PR #1. D-060 and the open question it answered existed only in local commits, so the second session never saw either — its D1 call was made in good faith without the user's answer, not against it. **The lesson is operational, not technical: an unpushed decision is a decision nobody else can see.**
  - **D1 — D-060 stands.** The user was asked again and kept it: `hard_stale` is tested first. The merged `hard-below-staleness-still-fresh` is gone; `hard-below-staleness` → `hard_stale` ships. The remote's paragraphs saying "a node never drops its peers while the network calls its view fresh" had auto-merged into `SPEC.md` §12.1 and into `GOSSIP.md` **outside any conflict marker**, textually clean, directly under the D-060 text they contradict. The citation guard caught the first; the second was found by reading, and now has a guard that looks for the rejected reading itself rather than for the accepted one's phrasing.
  - **D2 — the other session was right, and I was wrong.** `c2sp.org/tlog-policy` rules out only "multiple log lines **with the same public key**", so several `log` lines with distinct keys are grammatical — and §9.1 promises that grammar "verbatim … not redefined here". My 6.5l-iii at-most-one rule *was* a redefinition. It is removed from both parsers; `two-log-lines` MUST parse; the one-per-trust-file limit stays where it belongs, on the correspondence rule. The tests that asserted my answer now assert the upstream rule precisely: distinct keys parse, the same key twice is refused.
  - **D12 — the other session caught what I missed.** I placed each banned byte *between* fields, which rules out a reader that splits on it. A reader that keeps it as **content** sees `quorum\vpi` as one unknown keyword, refuses it for the wrong reason, and passes. Only a second copy inside a URL catches that reader. Adopted: every banned byte ships twice. The control shows it exactly — treating the bytes as content fails the three `-in-url` cases and **none** of the between-fields ones.
  - **Kept from the other session**: its `policy.py`, `bad-log-key-as-witness`, its `installs_test.go` and `proofs/cases.json` for D14 (compatible with the `proven/` bundles map here — same conclusion, two independent checks), its sharper `policies/` declarations — eleven cases that had pointed at the table's opening sentence now name their own row — and its §9.1 grammar table, which was better on every disputed row.
  - **And a bug of my own that only the merge exposed.** 6.5l-ii-b wrote "the states are tested in the order given" and "`hard_stale` is tested before `fresh`", and left §8.1's table listing `fresh` first. The section contradicted itself and every guard was green, because every guard checked for a phrase and none checked the order. The table is now in evaluation order, and `TestTheFreshnessTableIsInTheOrderItIsEvaluated` checks the rows — controlled by putting the old order back, which fails it.
  - **Mechanics.** Merged rather than rebased: two histories of the same checkpoints are the true record, and nine rebased commits would have meant resolving the same conflicts nine times. Every generated artifact was **regenerated** from the reconciled generators, never hand-merged. Three controls on the reconciled decisions, three reds, all through vector-driven tests. 770 tests; every gate green.

- **6.5l-vi-b-i — §10.1: fifteen refusal codes, in the order the verifier already checks them (D-061).**
  - **Specify what is true, not what would be tidy.** Before writing a row I traced the Go verifier's refusal order line by line: note framing, log signature, checkpoint body, origin, cosignatures, quorum, entry bundles, head, fork-then-rollback, tiles. §10.1 states exactly that order, so this step *describes* the implementation rather than changing it — 6.5l-vi-b-ii then makes each boundary observable.
  - **Twelve codes for the loop, three for a bundle.** A bundle's checkpoint is checked with the loop's codes (§8.1's condition 1 *is* §10's checkpoint check), plus `bundle`, `inclusion` and `binding` for its own steps. That matters because `proofs-bad/foreign-checkpoint` is where run 11 found signature-before-origin pinned only by a `says` string — so the bundle check had to be in the vocabulary for D-061 to reach it.
  - **One ordering needed an argument, not just a transcription.** §10's pseudocode lists fetching the entries *after* the fork comparison, but Go rebuilds and checks the head *before* it. Go is right: the fork comparison uses the served branch's root *recomputed from the served entries*, and if those do not hash to the head they were served with, a disagreement would accuse a log of signing two histories when it only served a broken one. §10.1 states the order and the reason, and says the pseudocode's placement is for readability.
  - **The step found a sentence of mine that had the order backwards.** §12.1's admission about a note with no signature lines said §10's signature check "is reached first". Go frames the note first — there is nothing to verify until the lines are found — and §10.1 now says so. With codes the two readings answer differently (`note` against `log-signature`), so the admission becomes a vector in 6.5l-vi-b-ii and leaves the list then; for now it says what is true.
  - **And a guard that passed against the sentence it was written to catch.** The negative check for that wrong sentence was green on the first run, because the sentence is wrapped across a line break in the source. The check now collapses whitespace first. Same failure family as the presence-anywhere needles — a phrase check proves less than it appears to.
  - **Two guards taught about the new codes.** The collision guard: no §10.1 code may also be a §5.2 code, because a reason code has to mean one thing wherever an operator reads it. And the bare-vector-name guard, which treated the backticked `log-signature` in §12.1 as a missing vector: it read §5.2's table shape only, and now reads §10.1's too, with a floor so it fails loudly if it ever stops seeing them.

- **6.5l-vi-b-ii-a — `internal/refusal`: §10.1's list as a type, and `tlog.Verify` says which of its three steps refused.**
  - **A leaf package, so everything that refuses can depend on it.** It knows fifteen names and their order and nothing about notes, logs or bundles. A refusal is `refusal.Wrap(code, err)`: the message and the chain stay the wrapped error's, so every `errors.Is(err, ErrLog)`, every `errors.As(err, &*ForkError)` the callers already rely on keeps working — a code is added to an error, never substituted for one. `CodeOf` reads the outermost code, because a refusal is coded once, at the step that made it.
  - **The list is closed in code as well as on paper.** `Wrap` panics on a code that is not on the list: a code nobody specified is a mistake in this repository, not a property of its input, and it should fail at the line that wrote it.
  - **One list, held to the document.** `specdoc`'s hand-kept copy of the fifteen became a view of `refusal.All()`, and a new test compares it with §10.1's two tables in full order. The existing test only checked the loop's twelve, so it could not see the bundle's three move: the controls (package swaps `bundle`/`inclusion`; document swaps the `inclusion`/`binding` rows) each failed the new test and left the old one green.
  - **`tlog.Verify` is §10.1's steps 2–4**, in the order of dependence: framing (`note`), the log's signature (`log-signature`), the body (`checkpoint`). The test classifies every file in `checkpoints-bad/` by §10.1's definitions — including the nine whose defect is a later step's and which this function must therefore *pass* — and iterates the directory, so a fixture added in ii-d cannot land without somebody deciding which step refuses it. `03-wrong-origin` is signed as `norn/elsewhere`, so it is a `log-signature` refusal here: the signature decides before the origin, which is the ordering ii-d makes a black-box vector.
  - **A malformed trusted key carries no code.** Left to `note.Verify`, a bad key in the caller's configuration came back as the checkpoint's `log-signature` — or as `note`, if the checkpoint was also malformed. Both are wrong: neither is the input's fault. The keys are parsed first and the error is returned bare.
  - **Four controls, one per rule**, each failing exactly its rule's fixtures: without the framing step the six malformed notes fall through to `log-signature` (the very confusion §12.1's admission describes); with the signature step mislabelled, the four signature fixtures; with the body step mislabelled, the three body fixtures; without the key pre-parse, the configuration test, twice.

- **6.5l-vi-b-ii-b — every refusal in the verifier loop carries its §10.1 code, and the step found a site that gave the wrong one.**
  - **Two helpers, one rule.** `refuse(code, …)` is a log error with the code of the step that refused; `carry(cause, …)` keeps a code a lower layer already gave — `tlog` and `cosign` make their own checks and name them. What is wrong with the *anchor* (no log key, no genesis pin, no policy, a policy that does not parse, a witness given a note key) goes through `logErrorf` alone: still `ErrLog`, because the log cannot be used, but uncoded, because §10.1's codes describe what was served. A fork is `refusal.Wrap(Fork, &ForkError{…})`; every caller already finds it with `errors.As`, never a type assertion, so the agent's evidence path is untouched.
  - **`cosign.Verify` names its own step**, as `tlog.Verify` did in ii-a: `cosignature` for a listed witness's line that does not verify, `note` for a note that does not frame. A witness key that does not parse, or is a plain note key, is the policy's mistake — it is now checked before the note is read and carries no code. `proof.Check` calls the same function, so ii-c inherits this.
  - **The finding: a short bundle was refused as `unserved`.** `fetchEntries` counted entries across bundles, reading on until `size` had arrived. A bundle that framed and held one entry too few therefore sent it after `tile/entries/001.p/1` — a file no checkpoint of 127 implies — and it refused `unserved` a log that had served every file it was asked for. §10.1 step 8 says `entries`: "the entry bundles the checkpoint's size implies … do not add up to that size". The rule is now positional — every bundle but the last holds 256, the last holds the rest, anything else is `entries` — and the empty-bundle and final-count checks it subsumes are gone. The red run showed the old answer exactly: `fetching entry bundle 1: … 001.p/1: no such file`.
  - **A second, smaller one: a bundle that did not frame was not even `ErrLog`.** `DecodeBundle`'s error was returned raw. It is `entries` now, and `ErrLog` like every other refusal.
  - **An over-determined test, caught by its own name.** `TestVerifyChecksTheHeadAgainstTheEntries` edited the root line of the honest signed head. That is a `log-signature` refusal two steps before the head check, and it passed from the day it was written because it asked only for an error (D-044's failure, in this package's own suite). It now signs and cosigns the wrong root, as a lying operator would, and asserts `head`.
  - **Codes alone do not pin an order.** A case with one fault answers the same whichever order the checks run in, so `TestTheEarlierStepDecides` has five two-fault logs where §10.1's earlier step must decide: origin before cosignature, cosignature before quorum, quorum before entries, rollback before tiles, and **head before fork** — §10.1's argued case, a corrupt branch that must be refused for its head rather than raise an accusation against a log that signed one history. Signature before origin is a single-fault case in the main table (the right key under another origin's name, with the anchor pinning that other origin too).
